(2) A trial is a real fit, so 50 trials across four families is 200 fits: the slow tests that run on plan §10's one-minute budget now override `tuning_trials: 5` (the schema floor) alongside the `ensemble`/`folds` overrides they already carried, for the same reason those exist. *Consequences:* the control does what it says; a default run now searches hyperparameters rather than taking the preset's defaults, which costs time within the configured `time_limit` and is what the setting was always meant to buy; `tuning_trials` is part of the recipe hash, because it genuinely changes the model.
- **DEC-074 — A setting no stage reads is marked `advisory`, rendered disabled, and left out of the recipe hash.** *Context:* after DEC-073 wired `tuning_trials`, nine settings remained that the schema carries and no stage reads: the whole `features` block (`auto_feature_engineering`, `categorical_encoding`, `numeric_scaling`, `text_columns`, `selection`, `max_features`), `monitoring.retraining`, `monitoring.performance_alert_drop_pct` and `governance.retention_days`. They were live, editable controls. The Setup screen offered "Retraining: weekly" and "Categorical encoding: one-hot" and honoured neither, and `governance.retention_days` — a data-protection promise — was documented as "recorded, not enforced" in one paragraph of `docs/AWS_DEPLOYMENT.md` and nowhere the person setting it would look. Deleting them was the other option and was rejected: the shapes are agreed product intentions, `docs/AWS_DEPLOYMENT.md` §7 already describes what Phase 4 does with three of them, and removing them would lose that agreement and the run records that carry it. *Decision:* one set, `engine.config.ADVISORY_PATHS`, is the single place the fact is written. `_numbered` — the one choke point every `FieldSpec` passes through — derives `FieldSpec.advisory` and the on-screen note from it, so a setting cannot be parked in the form and still be counted elsewhere. The form renders an advisory control disabled under `ADVISORY_NOTE` ("Coming later — recorded with the run, not yet applied"); disabling is what makes it inert rather than decoration, because a disabled control fires no `change` event and so its value never becomes a run override. `Recipe.recipe_hash` removes the same paths before hashing: the hash answers "would this produce the same model", and a setting no stage reads cannot change a model, so leaving them in made two runs that fitted byte-identical models hash differently because someone moved a control that does nothing. They remain *on* the recipe, and so in `run_config.json` and the run manifest — what the user chose is still worth recording; it is simply not part of the recipe's identity. *Consequences:* adding a path to `ADVISORY_PATHS` parks a setting and removing one ships it, with nothing else to change in either direction; the screen no longer promises behaviour the engine lacks; and the recipe hash means what it says. `tests/unit/test_advanced_settings_schema.py` pins both directions — the exact parked set, and that no setting a stage really reads is in it.
- **DEC-075 — One `Settings`, built from the environment on every read, is the only place a variable is named.** *Context:* three modules read `os.environ` directly - `engine/config.py` for `MARKETING_AI_CONFIG_DIR`, `engine/storage.py` and `engine/registry.py` for `MARKETING_AI_DATA_DIR` - and Phase 4a adds a second implementation of the store, the job runner and the metadata database, each with connection details of its own. Deducing which backend a process is running from which variables happen to be exported does not survive that. *Decision:* `engine/settings.py` holds one frozen pydantic `Settings` with the four switches (`storage_backend`, `job_backend`, `metadata_backend`, `llm_backend`), `aws_region` and the connection fields each backend needs; `ENV_VARS` maps field to variable so documentation, tests and readers cannot disagree; `_REQUIRED_FOR` refuses a backend whose fields are missing, at construction, naming the variable to export. `settings()` reads the environment on **every** call rather than caching, because the three reads it replaces did, and a test that patches a variable expects the next call to see it. The module imports nothing from `engine`, so `engine.config` stays the root of the import graph. Every default reproduces Phase 1 exactly: `local`, `thread`, `sqlite`, `fake`, `data/`, two workers. `data_dir` is deliberately *not* resolved, because it was relative to the working directory before and resolving it here would be a behaviour change dressed as a refactor; `config_dir` is resolved, because `config_root` resolved it. *Consequences:* one file answers "what does this process talk to"; all four switches are read and none is yet branched on, because `S3Storage`, `SageMakerJobRunner`, `PostgresMetadata` and `BedrockLLMClient` do not exist and a branch on a value with nothing behind it is the promise plan §2.1.5 forbids; `DEFAULT_CONFIG_ROOT`, `DEFAULT_DATA_DIR` and `REGISTRY_FILENAME` keep their old import paths as re-exports, so no caller changed; and `postgres_dsn` is a `SecretStr`, so a stray log line or `repr()` prints a mask rather than a password.
- **DEC-076 — `LLMClient` is three methods, its fake is a hash, and no fake output may reach a screen.** *Context:* Phase 3a needs a language model behind a protocol with a local implementation (protocol rule 6), and the fast suite has to run entirely against that implementation. *Decision:* `engine/llm.py` defines `LLMClient` with `complete`, `embed` and `count_tokens` and nothing else - streaming, tool use and provider-shaped options are a provider leaking into the engine, and the two implementations this has to cover (a hash and Bedrock) agree on nothing beyond the three. `FakeLLMClient` derives every answer from a SHA-256 of the exact request, so the same call returns the same bytes on any machine, and records an `LLMCall` per request so a test can assert that a prompt was built once rather than per row. Its completions read `[fake completion <digest>]`: visibly machine-made, and not a plausible summary of anything. `usage_from` totals the recorded calls into the `LLMUsage` a manifest carries, counting `complete` and `embed` but not `count_tokens`, which measures a prompt rather than sending one. *Consequences:* Phase 3a writes `BedrockLLMClient` beside the fake and changes nothing else; the fake's embeddings support exactly two assertions - identical texts embed identically, different ones do not - and carry no semantics, which is stated where they are produced; and the rule that no fake completion may reach the UI is written at the seam rather than enforced there, because it cannot be: with `llm_backend=fake` a rendering path must be *unreachable*, not merely unused, and that is a question for the review of each Phase 3a path.
- **DEC-077 — The composite primary key is a widened type on the contracts and a refusal at the boundary, not a half-threaded feature.** *Context:* the shared contracts live in append-only files (protocol §4), so a branch that needed `primary_key` to hold several columns could not widen it later; the type had to be settled before the branches started. Threading a composite key through the stages is Phase 2 work, and `engine/stages/train.py`, `evaluate.py` and `explain.py` are frozen. Protocol §2 names three models - `RunRequest`, `RunConfig`, `RunManifest` - and locates all three in `engine/contracts.py`; this repository spreads them differently. `RunRequest` is in `api/schemas.py`, `RunManifest` is in `engine/contracts.py`, and there is no `RunConfig`: the run's reproducible configuration is `engine.config.Recipe` (the object `RunManifest.recipe` carries and `train(recipe)` takes), while `run_config.json` is `ResolvedConfig`, the merged YAML document, whose leaves are config settings and not per-run choices. `Recipe` is therefore what §2 means by `RunConfig`, and it already carried a `primary_key` to widen. *Decision:* `PrimaryKey = str | list[str]` in `engine/config.py`, on `RunRequest`, `Recipe`, `RunRecord`, `FeatureSchema` and the new `RunManifest.primary_key`. `RunRecord` and `FeatureSchema` are not in §2's list and are widened anyway: the same column name is serialised into both, and both live in an append-only file, so leaving them narrow would stop the key at the first artefact it reaches with no way for Phase 2 to fix it. `key_columns()` is the accessor - it returns a tuple whether the value was spelled as one name or several, so no caller needs an `isinstance` check - and `sole_key()` narrows, raising `COMPOSITE_KEY_NOT_SUPPORTED` with what to do instead. `POST /runs` narrows once, at the top of the handler, and the score job narrows once more from the run record. `StageContext.primary_key` stays `str`: widening it would push `sole_key()` into about fifteen call sites across stages Phase 2 does not own and three that are frozen, which is a shared file rewritten by the branch that needs the least of it. This is the one place the codebase's "collections are tuples" rule yields (`engine/contracts.py`'s docstring): three branches are written against the spelling `str | list[str]`, and the serialised form is a JSON array either way. *Consequences:* every contract a composite key travels through already accepts one, and none of them has to change again; a composite key today gets a named error saying what to do rather than a silent join on the first column, which would report success on the wrong rows; and Phase 2 needs one ruling from the human before it can finish - whether it may widen `StageContext` and the `sole_key()` call sites in `engine/pipeline.py` - which is the open item in `docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md`.
- **DEC-078 — `dataset_id` and `client_id` are on the request and refused by it.** *Context:* protocol §2 puts both on `RunRequest` and `RunManifest` for Phase 2, and nothing in this phase can resolve either: there are no clients, no sources and no onboarded datasets. *Decision:* the fields exist, with `None` as the default, and `POST /runs` answers `422 DATASET_ONBOARDING_NOT_AVAILABLE` when either is set, naming the alternative (`upload_id`). Accepting them and ignoring them was rejected: the request also carries an `upload_id`, so an ignored `dataset_id` means the run scores a different file from the one the caller asked for and says nothing. *Consequences:* the shape is settled for the branches that need it; the engine never half-honours a request; and when onboarding lands, Phase 2 deletes one guard rather than changing a contract.
- **DEC-079 — Marker blocks are in the shared files, and a test fails when one goes missing.** *Context:* protocol §4 gives every shared file three append-only blocks, and a convention nothing checks is a convention that lasts until the first hurried merge. *Decision:* the blocks are in all twelve shared files, and `tests/unit/test_shared_file_markers.py` asserts that each file carries all three, exactly once, in order. Two deliberate departures from §4's list. `docs/API.md` gets none: it is generated and `make lint` fails on a hand-edit (DEC-014), so a block there would be deleted by the next `make generate`. `ui/modules/router.js` did not exist - Phase 1's router is `ui/app.js` - so it was written as a registry: a phase module calls `registerModule({name, routes, render})`, `app.js` asks `resolveRoute()` before its own routing and falls through when nothing claims the hash, which with no module registered is every hash. `api/main.py` gained `PHASE_ROUTERS`, a list a branch appends to from its own block, because `ALL_ROUTERS` is a closed tuple in `api/routes/__init__.py` and three branches cannot each edit it. *Consequences:* the protocol's §4 is enforced rather than remembered; the UI behaves exactly as before, since the registry is empty; and `tests/integration/test_ui.py` now resolves nested imports and checks `ui/modules/router.js` is served, so the new file is covered by the same invariant as the old ones rather than exempt from it.
- **DEC-080 — Plan §11's acceptance criterion is tested by driving the real screens in a browser.** *Context:* plan §11 does not end with an endpoint, it ends with a sentence about a person: "a non-technical user downloads the template, fills it with real historical data, uploads it, keeps every default, clicks Run, and receives a scored file with reasons and actions for a second upload, without anyone from the ML team touching it." Every integration test before this one drove `Pipeline` or the API in process, and `tests/integration/test_ui.py` checks the screens' wiring without ever opening them, so the suite could be green while that sentence was false — and it was. An API-level test could not have closed the gap either: `POST /runs` returning `{run_id}` is a success at that level even when the job it submitted stops at `prepare`, and the second defect the journey found exists only because of a form field the *browser* omits, which no in-process caller would omit. *Decision:* `tests/integration/test_acceptance.py` starts the real app under uvicorn, opens `/ui` in Chromium through playwright and walks the sentence as clicks and file inputs — Overview, the use-case card, the Setup screen's own template link, the upload, Run, the Running screen, Results, the Data / Model / Output pages, "Score new data", and the `scores.csv` link — making no request to the API itself, so every step is something a user does. It carries `pytest.mark.slow` and `pytest.mark.integration` and sits behind a module-level `pytest.importorskip("playwright.sync_api")` and a `chromium.launch` guarded into `pytest.skip`. The marker is plan §10's reason — a journey that trains a model is minutes, not seconds — and the skip is a second one: `ci.yml` runs the fast suite on a machine with no browser, and a test that goes red over a missing browser teaches people to read red as noise. Skipping cleanly there and running under `nightly.yml`'s `make test-all` puts the signal where someone acts on it. **One thing differs from a real user's run, deliberately: the compute budget.** The shipped default is `model_search.time_limit_minutes: 30` (`configs/engine.yaml`), which no test can wait for, so the app is started against a *copy* of `configs/` whose `targeted_advertisement.yaml` appends a one-minute budget and `tuning_trials: 5` — the same two values, for the same reason, that `tests/integration/test_train_flow.py`'s OVERRIDES already carries (that one also drops `strategy` to `fast`; this run leaves `strategy` at its default, because the journey is about defaults). The edit is made before the server starts, in a file no screen exposes, so it is a test budget and not a default the user changed: on the screens every default is kept, Advanced settings is asserted closed, and the primary key, target and problem type are whatever detection proposed. "Real historical data" is not available offline, so the two uploads are the committed generator's `clean` and `scoring` variants (`tests/fixtures/make_data.py`). *Consequences:* the test fails today and is kept failing rather than trimmed to what the product does — the journey stops at the Run button on the product's own "Preparing features is not built yet.", which is `api/routes/runs.py` submitting DEC-060's M2 placeholder for every training run (the placeholder its own entry says M3 replaces wholesale), and behind that `ui/api.js`'s `postUpload` never sending `mode`, so a "Score new data" upload is refused with `UPLOAD_MODE_MISMATCH`; a failing test that asserts the plan is the honest record of a Phase 1 criterion that does not hold, and trimming it would have converted a product defect into a documented feature. All 13 cases share one module-scoped journey fixture, so a stop anywhere in the walk reports as 13 errors rather than one — the cost of paying for the journey once instead of thirteen times. And plan §11's criterion now has exactly one place that answers it, so whether Phase 1 is finished stops being an argument about what the rest of the suite covers.
- **DEC-081 — `POST /runs` chooses its job by the run's mode, and the train flow is the one a training run gets.** *Context:* plan §11's acceptance test had never been run, and driving it in a browser stopped on the product's own words: "Run failed · Preparing features is not built yet." The route chose between two job builders on `if version is None`, and `version` is only ever assigned on the score path (`score_version(...)`, inside `if body.mode is RunMode.TRAIN: ... else:`). So the test was true for *every* training run, and every training run got DEC-060's `build_m2_job` — the milestone-2 placeholder that replays ingest and validate and then stops at `prepare` with `STAGE_NOT_IMPLEMENTED`. `Pipeline.run_train` had worked since M3 and was simply never reached from the product: run in process on the same configuration and data it takes 42 s and returns ROC-AUC 0.897. The suite did not catch it because `test_m2_job_runs_two_stages_then_fails_honestly_at_prepare` asserted the failure, so 2,699 fast tests and 94 slow tests were green over a product that could not train. A second defect sat behind it: `ui/api.js` never sent `mode` with an upload, `POST /uploads` defaults it to `train` and `POST /runs` refuses a mismatch, so "Score new data" failed on the Run click with `UPLOAD_MODE_MISMATCH` — advice the screen could not act on, since uploading again is what had just failed. *Decision:* the route dispatches on `body.mode`, which is the thing that actually decides the flow; `build_train_job` is the mirror of `build_score_job` and submits `Pipeline.run_train`; `postUpload` takes the mode and `usecase.js` passes the one it already holds. `build_m2_job` is kept but unreachable from the route, because a stage that genuinely cannot run should stop with a code and a sentence rather than raising, and that behaviour is worth keeping tested for the next stage that needs it. The test that asserted the bug is replaced by `test_a_training_run_submits_the_train_flow`, which asserts the builder the route submits — the assertion whose absence let this ship — and the API module now stands in for the train flow the way it already stands in for ingest and validate, so the fast suite does not fit a model per test. *Consequences:* the product can train; plan §11's acceptance test passes end to end in a browser (DEC-080); and the lesson is recorded where it happened — a test that asserts a placeholder's behaviour keeps the placeholder alive long after the real thing lands, so the assertion has to be about what the caller *chooses*, not about what the callee currently does.
- **DEC-082 — Integrating Phase 2 with the trunk: one run lifecycle, and a dataset is an `UploadInfo`.** *Context:* Phase 4a moved run creation, job specs and job bodies into `engine/runs.py` (DEC-327) while Phase 2 extended the old copies in `api/routes/runs.py` to accept a `dataset_id` (DEC-107); merging both kept two lifecycles, and the second never got a job spec, so a dataset run could not run in a container. Three further places assumed every run has an upload: the win-back and root-cause modules re-read `upload_key(record.upload_id, ...)`, and the Phase 4a run index requires `upload_id`. *Decision:* the copies in `api/routes/runs.py` are deleted. `_DatasetSource` satisfies `UploadInfo` (its `upload_id` is the dataset id, its `source_key` the dataset frame), and the provenance a dataset adds travels as a `DatasetLineage` argument to `create_run`, which sets `dataset_id`, `client_id` and `dataset_fingerprint` and leaves `upload_id` null. Reading a run's rows back goes through `engine.onboarding.datasets.run_source_key`, which answers the upload's source file or the dataset frame and refuses a record naming neither. The run index projects the dataset id into its `upload_id` column rather than making the column nullable, which would need a migration for no reader that tells the two apart. DEC-078's guard is gone, as its consequences said it would be; DEC-107's four refusals stay. *Consequences:* one code path creates and submits every run; a dataset run gets a `job_spec.json` and can run on SageMaker like an upload; the generative stages work on a dataset run; and composite keys are still refused with 501 until `StageContext.primary_key` is ruled on.

### Plan A — Phase 2 completion and integration hardening (DEC-083 … DEC-099)

Plan A (`docs/plans/MARKETING_AI_PLAN_A_PHASE2B.md`) assumes seven rulings, D1 to D7. Each is recorded here in M34, as the plan asks; the milestone that implements it is named in the entry.

- **DEC-083 — (D1) `StageContext.primary_key` is `list[str]`; a composite key travels as its columns and one joined row key.** *Context:* a periodic dataset has one row per entity per snapshot date, keyed `(entity_key, snapshot_date)`; the stages carried a single key column, so DEC-107 refused such a dataset with a 501. *Decision:* `StageContext.primary_key` is a list, and a single string is normalised to a one-item list in `__post_init__`, so every Phase 1 caller is unchanged. A new `engine/keys.py` holds the one normalisation: stages that *exclude* columns (validate, prepare's reserved set, the schema, the drift baseline, the recipe) receive every key column; stages that *identify* rows (explain's `row_explanations.parquet`, the reason join, the scoring summary) receive a single row key, which for one column is the column itself and for a composite key is `_row_key`, the parts as text joined by `|`, added after validation and never a feature. `scores.csv` writes each original key column separately, as the text read from the uploaded rows by row key, so ids keep their leading zeros and a date reads as the date. Artefacts store the key through `normalise_key`, a bare `str` for one column, so a Phase 1 `run.json`, recipe and recipe hash are byte-identical. `validate` checks the tuple (`PK_NOT_UNIQUE` on the pair, `PK_NULLS` when any part is empty, `PK_MISSING` naming the missing part) under the existing codes; the plan's `PK_HAS_NULLS` is the existing `PK_NULLS`, because the 19 Phase 1 codes are a regression gate. A composite key splits by entity (`split.group_column = <entity key>`), or on the snapshot date when the use case is time-based, recorded as `derived` in `run_config.json`; the API applies this before validation and `StageContext` again, idempotently. `actions` takes an `entity_key`: a customer suppressed at any snapshot is suppressed at all of them, and the control group draws customers rather than rows. Uploads keep a single key (`sole_key`); only a dataset carries a composite one. Two existing tests change, both because D1 changes the behaviour they pin: `test_runs_from_dataset.py`'s 501 test now asserts the run is accepted, and `test_runs_module.py` reads `ctx.primary_key` as `["customer_id"]` (and `ctx.key` as `"customer_id"`). *Consequences:* supersedes DEC-107's `DATASET_COMPOSITE_KEY_NOT_WIRED` 501, which is removed; `train.py`, `evaluate.py` and `explain.py` are unchanged, because they select features by the recipe and read the key they are given. The generative win-back and root-cause modules still take a single key (`sole_key`) and refuse a composite run by name.
- **DEC-084 — (D2) The six inactive feature settings stay in the schema, disabled, and out of the recipe hash.** *Context:* `features.categorical_encoding`, `numeric_scaling`, `text_columns`, `selection`, `auto_feature_engineering` and `max_features` are in the advanced-settings schema but nothing reads them. *Decision:* they stay, rendered disabled with "Coming later", and are excluded from the recipe hash so two runs differing only in them share a recipe. Implemented in M38. *Consequences:* no setting pretends to do something; building them later is a schema change rather than a new field.
- **DEC-085 — (D3) `configs/industries/` holds one file per industry.** *Context:* the library added industries outside `configs/`, and two tests assert exactly one industry. *Decision:* a second industry file is allowed; the tests validate every file instead. Implemented in M38.
- **DEC-086 — (D4) Phase 4a's one-line change to the frozen `train.py` is approved, conditionally.** *Context:* Phase 4a added an artefact upload to `engine/stages/train.py`, a frozen file. *Decision:* approved, on condition that a test proves local behaviour unchanged when the storage backend is local; moving the call into `ModelRegistry.register()` is preferred when it is clean. Which option was taken is recorded when M38 lands.
- **DEC-087 — (D5) Free-text PII is detected at profiling and redacted wherever a sample is shown.** *Context:* DEC-110 documented that the whole-cell detectors miss contacts inside free text. *Decision:* text columns are scanned by substring at profiling; any sample shown in the UI or logs, and any LLM input, is redacted; the check `PII_IN_FREE_TEXT` is a warning, not an error. Implemented in M36. *Consequences:* closes the gap DEC-110 recorded, without changing what Phase 1 columns are dropped or redacted.
- **DEC-088 — (D6) One PII detector module, one pattern set.** *Context:* `engine.stages.ingest.detect_pii` and `engine.stages.prepare._detect_pii` disagree (library finding). *Decision:* a single `engine/pii.py` used by both call sites. Implemented in M36.
- **DEC-089 — (D7) One `FakeLLMClient` with modes; one check-result type.** *Context:* Phase 3a added a second fake LLM client, and Phase 2's `OnboardingCheck` twins Phase 1's check type (DEC-101). *Decision:* keep one `FakeLLMClient` with modes, and merge the check-result types into the Phase 1 contract. Implemented in M38.
- **DEC-090 — Onboarding reaches the Setup form through two registry hooks; next month is a replay of the model's own recipe.** *Context:* Plan A M35 (Phase 2 M13). The raw-tables panel (`ui/modules/onboarding/`) was built but nothing mounted it (CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS "the onboarding panel is built, nothing mounts it", items 1-2). Step 1 of the Setup form and the page header belong to Phase 1 files (`ui/usecase.js`, `ui/dom.js`). `tests/integration/test_ui.py` requires every import of those files to stay inside the Phase 1 modules plus `ui/modules/router.js`. Score mode needed a way to put next month's files through last month's decisions, with review only where the files changed. *Decision:* (1) The PHASE-2 block of `ui/modules/router.js` gains two hooks, `registerSetupSource`/`setupSource` and `registerHeaderTool`/`headerToolHtml`, plus a `MODULES_CHANGED` event. `usecase.js` offers the registered source's card beside "Upload a prepared file" and mounts it on demand. `dom.js` `pageHead` draws the header tool beside the logo. `app.js` redraws on the event and now draws one route at a time, so a module's redraw cannot paint over a newer route. With nothing registered every screen renders as before. (2) "Use this dataset" hands Step 2 `{datasetId, clientId, primaryKey, target, problemType, timeColumn, manifest, sample}`, and Run posts `dataset_id` + `client_id`. For a periodic dataset in training it also sets the split to time-based on the snapshot date, as the reference prototype's `useDataset()` does. The split type and value are taken from the time-column field's `visible_when` in the advanced-settings schema, and the time column is overwritten rather than kept from an earlier upload. What was written is restored to the use case's own starting values when Step 1 goes back to a prepared file. So that such a split resolves for a use case whose prepared-file template has no date column (telco-churn's Kaggle file), `UseCaseConfig`'s template rule for `split.time_column` also accepts `standard_schema.snapshot_column` when the use case has a standard schema. Any other column still needs a template column with role `time`, and whether an upload has the column is still `check_time_column_missing`'s job. (3) The header's client defaults to "Demo", created once under the fixed id `c_demo` by `POST /clients/default` (`LocalClientStore.ensure_client`) in the industry the overview opens on. (4) Score mode replays the recipe the chosen model was trained with, found via model version → training run → dataset manifest → spec. `POST /clients/{id}/onboarding-specs/{sid}/replay` (`engine/onboarding/replay.py`) matches new files to the recipe's by confirmed role, then file name, then detected role. It copies each mapping unchanged and confirms each file's role. A file missing a column the mapping read has its mapping saved without that column and is the only file whose mapping step reopens; the user's saved fix is passed back in `mapping_ids` and used as is. A table the recipe reads but this month lacks is named. Only when nothing is missing is a re-pointed recipe saved, built with `mode: score`. The replay's messages are worded server-side. No new check codes were added, because `ONBOARDING_VALIDATION_CODES` is pinned to plan §7. (5) The Data page shows the lineage block from `GET /datasets/{id}/lineage`, which serves the engine's existing `lineage()` tree. *Consequences:* Plan A section 6's journey runs in the browser (`tests/integration/test_onboarding_acceptance.py`), and its training run is split by snapshot date (train, then validation, then test, in date order). A user who switches the split back to random still gets each customer's rows kept together (DEC-083). The time-based split has no gap between a training row's label window and the validation snapshots; that is an open item. No Phase 1 module imports onboarding. The Plan A sentence says three tables but the test uploads four, because the default churn label reads the activity table. Monthly scoring adds a copy of every table to the client, so the upload route's `max_sources` check counts only the client's tables that no saved recipe reads (`api.routes.sources.working_sources`); counting every file ever uploaded refused a four-table client's third month. The per-build `TOO_MANY_SOURCES` check is unchanged. The "Demo" client is created in the industry the overview opens on (`DEFAULT_INDUSTRY` first, M38), not the first industry file by name, which since M38 is `ad_tech`. *Addendum (merge with Phase 4b Part 1):* `modules/production/boot.js` reaches `dom.js` while `router.js` is still evaluating, and DEC-790 forbids a path from boot back to the router, so `dom.js` no longer imports `headerToolHtml` from the router. It keeps the header slot itself (`setHeaderTool`, `headerToolHtml`), `registerHeaderTool` fills it, and the router's `headerToolHtml` reads it, so the seam's API is unchanged. `test_setup_wiring_ui.py` pins the reversed edge in its own test. The client picker now reads `GET /clients` first and sends `POST /clients/default` only when the list is empty: the `POST` writes, is audited and needs the Analyst role (`api.access_policy`), so a Viewer can still pick a client and no page load after the first writes anything.
- **DEC-091 — `prepare` converts nullable-integer columns to float before writing a clip bound or a fill value.** *Context:* A dataset built from raw tables stores count features as pandas' nullable integers, because a customer with no rows in a window has no count. Parquet reads them back as `Int64`. The default `outliers: clip` wrote a float percentile bound into the column, and `Int64` rejects floats (`TypeError: Invalid value '455.96' for dtype 'Int64'`), so the first training run on the first built dataset failed at the split stage. A float fill median had the same problem, on both the training path and scoring `replay`. *Decision:* `engine/stages/prepare.py` gains `_widened()`, which casts an extension-integer column to `float64` just before `clip` or a numeric `fillna`, in `_apply_outliers`, `_apply_missing_values` and `replay`. Any other dtype is returned unchanged. *Consequences:* Built datasets train and score. A CSV upload's NumPy `int64` columns go through the same `clip` as before, so Phase 1 recipes and transforms are unchanged; `tests/unit/test_prepare_nullable_integers.py` pins both behaviours. `prepare.py` is not in the frozen list.
- **DEC-092 — (D6) One PII detector, one pattern set, one rule: `engine/pii.py`.** *Context:* `engine.stages.ingest.detect_pii` (read by the validation report) and `engine.stages.prepare._detect_pii` (which actually redacted or dropped columns) were separate tables that disagreed. Prepare examined every column type, matched on a header alone and used a phone regex that matches ISO dates, so on `library/online-retail` it redacted `snapshot_date` as a phone number while `validation.json` said nothing. *Decision:* `engine/pii.py` is the only detector. A header matching a detector's column-name pattern fires on its own, whatever the column's type or values. These are old prepare's `_PII_NAME` tokens: email/mail, phone/mobile/msisdn/telephone, name/surname, pan, aadhaar/aadhar, and the name-only kinds address/street/postcode/zipcode, ssn and passport. So a `phone` column pandas reads as float64, an upper-case `customer_name` or a hashed `email` column is still redacted. Without such a header only the values count. `VALUE_DETECTORS` (email, phone, pan, aadhaar, name) keep ingest's calibrated whole-cell rates and open-vocabulary guard. Their patterns are the union of the legitimate shapes both old tables knew, without the shapes that were never personal data: ISO dates, date-times and bracket noise. Ingest's type gate applies to that values-alone branch: only STRING, TEXT and INTEGER are examined, and digit-shaped kinds only for INTEGER. `ingest.detect_pii`/`PII_DETECTORS` re-export it, prepare calls it per column with `ingest.infer_column_type`, and `engine.generative.redaction` delegates to it. Removing the false date flag showed that it had also kept the as-of date out of every model. Prepare now does this on purpose: a column named like a `time_column_hints` entry whose values are dates is carried with the rows and never trained on. `prepare.json` names it under the new optional `carried_columns` (`CarriedColumn`, reason `snapshot_date`), which the Running line and the Data preparation page show. Replay leaves it in the frame. A constant one is still dropped as constant first. *Consequences:* a column is redacted exactly when `PII_DETECTED` names it, and the merged detector is identical to or stricter than each old one. On all five library samples both call sites flag nothing, which matches the validation report, and online-retail loses only the false `snapshot_date` flag. Every shipped use case trains on exactly the columns it did before. A header such as `street_address`, `product_name` or `email_sent_date` is now reported as well as redacted, as prepare already redacted it. `tests/unit/test_pii.py` covers every value pattern and every header token of both old tables.
- **DEC-093 — Odd column names get safe internal names at ingest, used only inside the model boundary.** *Context:* the library could not put `default.payment.next.month` in a template and renamed dotted columns by hand. Measured on the credit-default sample: LightGBM refuses a feature name containing JSON special characters and XGBoost refuses brackets, and AutoGluon silently skips both families ("No model was trained during hyperparameter tuning ... Skipping this model"). Validate, prepare, the split, evaluate and export work with any header. *Decision:* in `engine/column_names.py`, ingest builds a `ColumnNames` mapping for the file. A safe header (`^[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*$`) keeps its name. An unsafe one is transliterated and underscored, with `_2`, `_3` suffixes on a clash, so `default.payment.next.month` becomes `default_payment_next_month`, which is exactly the library's hand rename. The mapping is stored beside `scorer.json` as `model/column_names.json`, and only when it is not the identity. The pipeline hands `train.train` a translated recipe and partitions. Scorers gain `with_column_names`: they report and accept the client's headers and rename columns on the way into the predictor. evaluate, the champion re-score and `score.predict` use that scorer. Explain drives the predictor directly, so it runs on internal names, and its importance and reasons are translated back before they are written. `ingest.target_candidate` and the hint matching also match through the safe form. *Consequences:* every artefact a person reads, plus `scores.csv` and every reason, names columns as the uploaded file did, and no model family is lost to a header. Files with safe headers are byte-for-byte unchanged: identity mapping, no new file, `scorer.json` identical. The frames outside the model are not renamed: every stage there already handled any header, and renaming them would have meant translating every artefact back. A Hypothesis property test shows that arbitrary Unicode, dotted and spaced names round-trip. In a renamed run, `scorer.json`'s `recipe_hash` is the hash of the translated recipe.
- **DEC-094 — `threshold.mode: auto`, defined: the configured metric's optimum on validation, under a flagged-rate ceiling, else the top decile.** *Context:* on `library/online-retail` the F1 optimum called every row positive (recall 1.0, specificity 0.0), and the Model page would have shown 100 % recall. *Decision:* `engine.stages.scorer.choose_operating_point`, called by `choose_threshold`, takes the threshold that maximises the configured metric on the validation split. That is `model_search.metric` when a threshold can move it (F1, recall, precision), and F1 for roc_auc or pr_auc. Ties go to the lowest threshold, except for recall, where they go to the highest. The optimum is refused if it flags more than `evaluation.threshold.max_flagged_rate` of the validation rows, or flags every row. The threshold then becomes the top decile of the validation scores, or the top `max_flagged_rate` when the ceiling is below a tenth, so the fallback never flags more than the ceiling it enforces. If the top tie block is larger than that share, the fallback is that block; if every row has the same score, it is 0.50. The fallback is recorded as a `ThresholdFallback` in `scorer.json` (code `THRESHOLD_FALLBACK`, reason, optimum and fallback thresholds and flagged rates, ceiling), and omitted when unused. It is also stated in one sentence in `threshold_detail`, which `evaluation.json` and the Model page carry. The ceiling is 0.30 in `configs/engine.yaml` and can be changed there or per use case, but not through run overrides. A bare `ThresholdConfig()` built in code has no ceiling, which is the pre-M36 rule and is what keeps the scorer's existing unit tests valid. A threshold is never rounded above the score it cuts at, and flagged counts are taken at the stored value. *Consequences:* `evaluate.py` is untouched; its display string is generic and passes the sentence through. On the online-retail sample the baseline's optimum flagged 81 % of validation rows and now falls back to about 11 %. A recall-optimised use case will usually fall back, because maximum recall means flagging everyone.
- **DEC-095 — (D5) Free-text PII is detected at profiling, masked wherever shown, and reported as the extension code `PII_IN_FREE_TEXT`.** *Context:* the whole-cell detectors miss a contact typed inside a complaint (DEC-110). DEC-087 rules that this is a warning and does not change what is trained on. *Decision:* a column is free text when it is TEXT, or STRING averaging at least three words per value; this excludes codes and ids such as `ACC-1234567`. `engine.pii.free_text_pii` searches its sample for contacts (email, phone, pan, aadhaar; never names, DEC-213), and `ColumnProfile.free_text_pii_kinds` records the kinds found. Every surface that shows a cell replaces each contact with a `[REDACTED:kind]` marker before the cell is cut to length: profile sample values, top categories and preview rows; the sample values validation quotes; the onboarding dataset `sample.json`; and per-row reasons in the train and score flows. LLM inputs use the same redaction through `root_cause`. `validate` emits `PII_IN_FREE_TEXT` (warning, acknowledgeable, `details.pii_kinds`, no value). It runs from a new extension registry (`EXTENSION_CHECK_ORDER`/`EXTENSION_REGISTRY`), and the code lives in `engine.contracts.EXTENSION_VALIDATION_CODES`, which `ValidationCheck` and `OnboardingCheck` accept. *Consequences:* the Phase 1 table (`VALIDATION_CODES`, `CHECK_ORDER`, `CHECK_REGISTRY`) stays at nineteen codes. The column itself is kept and trained on as before. Regression test: Phase 2's synthetic complaints table (`make_raw` REMARKS), which exposed the gap. `configs/roles.yaml` no longer calls the column unredacted.
- **DEC-096 — The leak probe rebuilds features for the entities that can move, plus a control group, instead of the whole spine.** *Context:* `_leak_probe` re-dates 500 real rows per event table to after the last snapshot and rebuilds every feature, to catch a leak the SQL guard cannot see. It copied each event table with `pd.concat` to add those rows (five million usage rows to add 500 at the M14 size) and rebuilt every feature for every snapshot row. In effect that was a second full aggregation: 10.7 of 137.7 profiled seconds at 20,000 customers, and roughly the size of the whole features stage at full scale. *Decision:*
  - The future rows are stacked inside DuckDB as a `UNION ALL` view, with each column of the injected rows cast to the type DuckDB gave the client's own table. Without the cast, an object column that is empty in the injected rows would read as INTEGER. The queries see the same rows the concatenated copy gave them.
  - Features are rebuilt only for the snapshot rows of the entities that received a future row, plus up to `_PROBE_CONTROL_ENTITIES` (500) entities that received none.
  - The build's own features are cut to the same entities by key (`_probed_features`), never by snapshot-row position. A `derive` feature over an event table joins every snapshot row to every event of its role, so that frame has one row per event, not one per snapshot. A positional mask raised IndexError on it (found in review), and selecting by key reports it as FUTURE_EVENTS_LEAKED, as the whole-spine probe did. A unit test and an end-to-end flow test pin this.
  - Every compiled feature query and every `derive` joins an event to its own entity. So a value can only move for an injected entity, and all of those are rebuilt, with every row the build produced for them. For a missing time bound, a `derive` over the whole history or a widened snapshot frame, the verdict is the one the whole spine would give.
  - The control entities are there for a query that reads other entities' events. A test builds exactly that leak and shows the control entities catch it while the injected entities alone do not.
  - If no snapshot row matches an injected entity (for example, keys that pandas compares differently from DuckDB), the probe falls back to the whole spine and compares the whole features frame. It is never weaker than before.
  - FUTURE_EVENTS_LEAKED keeps its severity, its non-acknowledgeability and its row counts. Floats are still compared at DEC-109's tolerance.

  *Consequences:* the probe fell from 10.7 s to 0.6 s under the profiler and peak memory no longer includes a copy of each event table. The broken-time-bound flow test and the new derive flow test still fail the build. A leak that affects only entities that received no future rows, and only entities outside the 500 control entities, would not be seen. No compiled query can produce such a leak, and the old probe's advantage there was incidental rather than designed.
- **DEC-097 — Profile before optimising: the build was slow because it did work twice, not because of Parquet; COPY, in-DuckDB joins, per-role materialisation and Arrow were measured and not adopted.** *Context:* Plan A M37 expected Parquet writing and column renaming to dominate the 828.9 s M14 build. It proposed DuckDB `COPY ... TO ... (FORMAT PARQUET)`, renames in SQL, per-role joins inside DuckDB, not materialising a DataFrame per role, and Arrow zero-copy. Profiling at 20,000 customers / 500,000 usage rows / 12 snapshots / 60 features:
  - `to_parquet` took 0.65 of 137.7 s.
  - The dataset fingerprint (`ingest.canonical_chunk_bytes`) was computed twice, once in `write_frame` and once in `build_manifest`: 42.3 s.
  - The write stage re-read, re-parsed and re-fingerprinted every source through `reader.profile` just to get PII flags and fingerprints: most of the 36.2 s spent in 12 source reads.
  - Column types were inferred up to three times.

  *Decision:*
  - Each source is read once: `read_upload(..., keep_all_rows=True)` is a backward-compatible option on the Phase 1 reader, which also returns `ReadResult.all_rows`. Asking for it does not change `frame` or `fingerprint`, and tests pin that for CSV and Parquet above and below the cap.
  - `SourceReader` gains `read_profiled`, which returns every row plus exactly the profile `profile` returned. Every build uses it, previews included. A passing preview reaches the write stage, because its `sample.json` is redacted by the PII flags. So the mani… [truncated] **Addendum (the full-size measurement).** Measured at the target size on identical tables and one idle 4-CPU machine, the build went from 763.5 s (before M37) to 369.1 s, which is still over the 300 s target. The rest of the gap was the fingerprint canonicaliser, which renders every cell of about 40 million source rows and of the 2.4-million-row dataset on one core. `_ContentDigest` now renders chunks in a spawned process pool (every core but one; never for a one-chunk digest or on fewer than three cores) and hashes the rendered bytes in arrival order in the one sha256. The bytes hashed are the same bytes in the same order, so every fingerprint is identical by construction. This was checked at full size: the parallel build's fingerprint equals the in-process fingerprint of the same file. A worker failure or a pool that cannot start falls back to in-process rendering with one warning. Processes are used because pandas' CSV writer holds the GIL. Spawn is used, not fork, because a build runs on the API's job thread and forking a threaded process can deadlock. The build then took **275.9 s: the target is met**. The build process peaked at 8.3 GB, plus about 1 GB across three render workers, against 10.9 GB before M37. The dataset is unchanged: identical cells, floats within 4 × 10⁻¹⁶ (DEC-109). `docs/PERFORMANCE.md` §6 has the table.
- **DEC-098 — M38: how rulings D2, D3, D4 and D7 were implemented.** *Context:* DEC-084, DEC-085, DEC-086 and DEC-089 record the rulings; this entry records what building them found. *Decision:* **D2** needed no engine change: DEC-074 had already built the mechanism. `engine.config.ADVISORY_PATHS` marks the six settings advisory, the served schema carries that, `ui/settings.js` disables them, and `Recipe.recipe_hash` already dropped them, so DEC-084's "excluded from the recipe hash, implemented in M38" was in fact already true, and no stored hash changes. M38 closes three gaps. The screen no longer sends an advisory value (`overridesFrom` skips it). A folded stage whose fields are all advisory leads its summary with the "Coming later" note. Tests pin the hash property both on `Recipe` objects and across two real train flows. An advisory override sent straight to the API is still accepted and recorded, because refusing it would turn a request an older client can send into a 422 for a value that does nothing either way. **D3:** the library's four industries and four use cases are moved, byte for byte, into `configs/industries/` and `configs/use_cases/`, with templates regenerated into `templates/`, and `library/configs/` is removed. `GET /industries` names a `default_industry` (telecom) and lists it first, and the overview gains an industry selector (`#/industry/<id>`). A use case's back link returns to the industry that lists it, and Telecom's screens are unchanged. **D4:** the upload stays in `engine/stages/train.py`, the approved fallback, because neither move is clean. `ModelRegistry.register()` is ruled out because only `S3ModelRegistry` holds a storage, S3 storage with the SQLite registry is a supported and tested combination that would then never publish the predictor, and the registry cannot tell an unpublished predictor from a published one. The register stage is ruled out because it would leave the predictor only on the container's disk through evaluate and explain, the most memory-hungry steps. `tests/unit/test_train_local_mirror.py` proves D4's condition on local storage with a real fit: nothing uploaded, no store write, no file's bytes or mtime changed. It also proves that on a mirrored store the predictor is published once, after `save()` and before `scorer.json`. **D7:** `engine/llm.py` has one `FakeLLMClient` with a `mode` (`FakeLLMMode`). `DIGEST` is the default; `GROUNDED` is what the fake generative backend uses; `UNGROUNDED`, `PII`, `OVERLONG`, `BANNED`, `MALFORMED`, `REFUSING` and `FAILING_JUDGE` each break exactly one guardrail rule. `GroundedFakeLLMClient` is deleted. `engine.contracts.ValidationCheck` is the one check contract for `validation.json` and a build report. It accepts the 19 Phase 1 codes plus the onboarding codes, carries an optional `source_id`, never lets `FUTURE_EVENTS_LEAKED` be acknowledged, and `OnboardingCheck` is an alias of it. `VALIDATION_CODES` is still the 19. *Consequences:* existing tests changed only where a ruling changes what they pin, each named in the branch's commit: the two "exactly one industry" assertions (D3) and the class named in three `isinstance` checks and one OpenAPI key (D7). The request-log entries for D3, D4, D7 and the train.py line are resolved.
- **DEC-099 — M39: the README is checked against the tests, the nightly runs `main`, and the library runs on request.** *Context:* on 2026-09-23 the README's Phase 2 table still said M8–M12 were pending a day after they merged green. GitHub fires `schedule` only on the default branch, which is not `main`. *Decision:* `scripts/check_readme.py` runs in CI after the fast suite. It reads that run's JUnit report and fails when a milestone table marks a milestone pending (or not started, todo, planned) while every mapped test that ran passed. The mapping is the explicit `MILESTONE_TESTS` in the script. A mapping must name tests that fail when the milestone's whole definition of done is unmet, so M13 stayed unmapped until M35 mounted the panel in Setup. The check is one-sided: it can prove a "pending" false, never a "done" true. Skips and deselected `@slow` tests are not evidence, and a missing mapped path is an error rather than a pass. `nightly.yml` checks out `main` by name on the scheduled run; a manual run tests its own branch. A separate `library` job runs `make library-test` when the repository variable `NIGHTLY_LIBRARY_TESTS` is `true` or the manual run ticks it. Plan A's Makefile targets live at the end of the PHASE-2 block, since Plan A is the Phase 2 completion. `docs/plans/` holds Plan A; the Phase 2, 3a, 4a and Evolve plans were never committed and are listed as missing rather than reconstructed. *Consequences:* the nightly still waits on one owner action, switching the default branch to `main`. Each milestone must add its tests to `MILESTONE_TESTS` when it merges. A milestone whose only tests are `@slow` is not judged by CI.
- **DEC-800 — Integrating Plan A, Phase 3b and the Phase 4b foundation: blocks in arrival order, uplift on a single-column key read through `key_columns`, and the infra settings mirror.** *Context:* the trunk's hundred ran out at DEC-099, so this entry opens DEC-800…899 for the trunk (`PARALLEL_WORK_PROTOCOL.md` §4). Phase 3b was built on `8f0d358`, before Plan A M34 made `StageContext.primary_key` a list (DEC-083) and before Phase 4b claimed a block in every shared file. Merged as they stood, `engine/uplift/flow.py` indexed frames with that list, so every uplift run would have got a one-column frame where it expected the key column; its train flow also re-declared `_model`, which `_TrainFlow` now types as an `AutoGluonScorer` (DEC-093). Separately, Phase 4b's twelve new `Settings` fields were never copied into `infra/naming.py`, so `tests/infra/test_settings_contract.py` had failed on `main` since `9e6a6ff`. *Decision:* shared-file blocks come in the order they reached `main`: Phase 2, 3a, 4a, 4b, then 3b, which is also Phase 3b's own rule that its block comes last (DEC-600); the marker test pins that order. The uplift flows read the one customer column through `_customer_key(ctx, stage)`, which returns `ctx.key_columns[0]` and refuses a composite key with `COMPOSITE_KEY_NOT_SUPPORTED` at the stage that meets it, rather than reducing it to its first column. The three Phase 1 calls the uplift flow makes (`validate_for_training`, `recipe_from_config`, `register.feature_schema`) get `ctx.key`, which is a plain string for one column, so a single-key uplift run records exactly what it did before. The uplift train flow's model attribute is `_uplift_model`. `infra/naming.SETTINGS_FIELDS` gains Phase 4b's twelve fields in `ENV_VARS` order; the infrastructure only uses the list to decide which variables a task may set, so no stack changes. The Criteo files and four library READMEs point at `configs/` instead of the removed `library/configs/` (DEC-085). `load_yaml` parses with libyaml's `CSafeLoader` when PyYAML has it: with five industries `GET /industries` parsed 89 YAML files per request and took 1.1 s against 0.35 s before, long enough that the uplift index was still loading when its browser journey read it; every configuration file in the repository, the broken fixtures included, parses to the same objects either way, and the request now takes about 0.2 s. *Consequences:* single-key uplift runs behave byte-for-byte as they did on Phase 3b's branch; a periodic dataset can train a Phase 1 model on its two-column key but not an uplift model, and says so. Phase 4b's route-policy table covers no route yet, and will need entries for Plan A's and Phase 3b's routes as well as the older ones.
- **DEC-801 — Integrating Phase 4b Part 1 with Plan A and Phase 3b: a phase module's screen is drawn at once, Phase 3b's routes get roles, and two tests stop depending on timing.** *Context:* Phase 4b (M46–M49) reached `main` built on Plan A M34 only; Plan A M35–M39 and Phase 3b were on their own branches. Where they met: (1) Phase 4b's `router.js` block loads `production/boot.js`, which imports `dom.js`, while Plan A's `dom.js` imported the router - a cycle DEC-790 forbids. Plan A fixed that on its own branch while this integration was running (the DEC-090 addendum: `dom.js` keeps the header slot and imports nothing, and the router fills it), and registered roles for its own three routes; the integration takes Plan A's fix for Plan A's files. (2) Plan A's `app.js` draws one route at a time and queues the rest; Phase 4b re-routes a page reloaded on an admin screen by dispatching `hashchange`, which then waited behind an overview draw stuck on `GET /industries` (`tests/integration/production/ui/deeplink.test.mjs`). (3) Phase 3b's six uplift operations had no entry in Phase 4b's route table, and its middleware refuses a route with none, so with sign-in on every uplift screen would have been refused. *Decision:* (2) `app.js` draws a route a phase module claims immediately instead of queueing it; the uplift and production modules already guard their screens against a late Phase 1 paint, and Phase 1's own routes stay queued as Plan A made them. (3) The six uplift operations join `LEGACY_POLICIES` by DEC-716's rules: the reads are Viewer, `GET /runs/{run_id}/uplift/{name}` is an audited read like any run artefact, and starting a run, measuring a campaign and an off-policy evaluation are Analyst. Two tests changed without loosening an assertion: Phase 3b's Node wiring test stubs the few browser members Phase 4b's `boot.js` touches (a `window.fetch` answering 404, which reads as "no sign-in"; `addEventListener`; the app element's `parentNode` and `querySelectorAll`), separately from the `fetch` whose calls it asserts; and Phase 4b's anonymous rate-limit test holds the limiter's clock inside one minute, because the window is the wall-clock minute and a slow run that crossed a boundary reset the count. *Consequences:* every one of the 82 API paths has a role, so sign-in refuses nothing the merged product serves; a slow overview request no longer delays a module's screen; the page's module graph has no cycle.

### Plan D — leftovers and hardening (DEC-850 … DEC-899)
Plan D (`docs/plans/MARKETING_AI_PLAN_D_HARDENING.md`) assumes three rulings, R1 to R3. Each is recorded here, as the plan asks for M53; the milestone that implements it is named in the entry.

- **DEC-850 — (R1) DEC-096's narrowed future-data leak check is accepted, with conditions.** *Context:* DEC-096 narrowed the build's leak probe to the snapshot rows of the entities given future-dated events plus up to 500 control entities, and its consequences name the blind spot: a leak that moves only entities outside that set. Plan D §1 rules on it as R1. *Decision:* accepted with conditions. The narrowed check stays the default for builds; the **full** check runs in the nightly suite and in the golden tests; a `full_leak_check: true` option exists and is forced on for the first build of any new onboarding recipe; the build report says which check ran. The SQL guard assertion on every compiled query is unchanged. Implemented in M55 (DEC-870 … DEC-874). *Consequences:* a recipe's first build pays for the full check once, later builds of the same recipe keep DEC-096's speed, and the blind spot is covered nightly and by the golden tests rather than on every build.
- **DEC-851 — (R3) The consent salt is a secret: read through `engine/settings.py`, no default in code, and production refuses to start without it.** *Context:* every principal hash (consent ledger, erasure register, audit `principal_hash`) was salted with the client id, else `local` (DEC-705, DEC-733), and the client id is written on every artefact, so anyone holding a hash and a customer list could test ids against it. Plan D §1 rules on it as R3. *Decision:* the salt is a secret read through `engine/settings.py`, never a default in code, and startup refuses production mode without it. Implemented in M54 (DEC-860). *Consequences:* a deployment's salt is set once and never changed, because a new salt leaves every stored hash unmatchable (DEC-860). R3 takes DEC-851 and R2 DEC-852 because M57's documents (`docs/LIBRARY.md`, `library/criteo-uplift/run_report.md`) cited R2 as DEC-852 before these entries were written.
- **DEC-852 — (R2) Criteo is for internal validation only.** *Context:* the Criteo Uplift Prediction Dataset is licensed CC BY-NC-SA 4.0, which does not allow commercial use (`library/criteo-uplift/run_report.md`). Plan D §1 rules on it as R2. *Decision:* Criteo may be used for internal validation only. It never appears in demos, screenshots, sales material or the demo dataset (Plan E), and any run report is marked **internal validation, non-commercial licence**. Implemented in M57 (`library/criteo-uplift/run_report.md`, `docs/LIBRARY.md`). *Consequences:* no Criteo number may be quoted outside internal validation. M57's attempt was refused by the network (403 from `go.criteo.net` and the mirrors), so no Criteo run exists; the run is an owner action on a machine with open internet.
- **DEC-853 — Uplift reads its key through `engine.keys`; the entity is the key's first column.** *Context:* Plan B §3 allows one row per customer per snapshot, and Plan A M34 made two-column keys a trunk feature (DEC-083), but the uplift flows refused them (`COMPOSITE_KEY_NOT_SUPPORTED`, DEC-800), `UpliftRunRequest.primary_key` was one string and campaign results narrowed the run key with `sole_key`. *Decision:* uplift reads keys exactly as Phase 1's stages do. Every key column (and the joined `_row_key`) is reserved from the features (`reserved_feature_exclusions`); rows are *identified* by `StageContext.row_key` (hold-out keys, `holdout_fingerprint`, row explanations, the reason join, the scoring summary); treatment, the hold-out, the control group and suppression are decided per **entity**, which is `engine.keys.entity_column` — the key's first column, the same entity Phase 1's grouped split, `apply_actions(entity_key=...)` and the consent ledger use. `scores.csv` writes each key column separately as text (`keys.key_text`), as Phase 1's does; a one-column key is written exactly as before. `POST /uplift/runs` accepts `primary_key` as a list and applies `split_config_for_key` so `run_config.json` records the derived group column; `measure_incrementality` joins the outcomes file on every key column (each part through `key_text`, so a Parquet timestamp matches a CSV date), and `POST /runs/{id}/campaign-results` passes the run's whole key. `_customer_key` and the flow's `COMPOSITE_KEY_NOT_SUPPORTED` are removed. *Consequences:* a periodic uplift file trains, scores and is measured keyed by both columns; a single-key uplift run is unchanged (every existing uplift test passes untouched). Phase 1's `POST /runs` still takes a composite key only with a built dataset (DEC-083, pinned by `test_a_composite_primary_key_is_refused_by_name`), so a scoring file with several snapshots per customer is scored through a dataset; `POST /uplift/runs` still takes an upload, not a `dataset_id`.
- **DEC-854 — `TREATMENT_VARIES_WITHIN_ENTITY`: a customer is in one arm per campaign.** *Context:* with snapshots, the same customer appears several times; if they are treated at one snapshot and held out at another, the uplift estimate compares a customer with itself and the per-customer control group of scoring has no meaning. *Decision:* a seventh uplift check, `TREATMENT_VARIES_WITHIN_ENTITY` (error, blocking, **not** acknowledgeable), added to `UPLIFT_VALIDATION_CODES` and to `CHECK_ORDER` right after `TREATMENT_NOT_BINARY`. It runs only for a two-column key: it groups the 0/1 treatment by the entity column — and by `uplift.campaign_id_column` when that is configured and present, so a customer may be re-drawn between campaigns but not within one — and names how many customers are in both arms. When it fires, `TREATMENT_ARM_TOO_SMALL` and the randomness check are skipped ("skipped, not failed twice"): there are no per-customer arms to count or to predict. For a two-column key the arms are counted in **customers** (distinct entities, and distinct converting entities for the positives), which keeps the existing message ("the treated group has N customers") true; the randomness classifier is cross-validated with `StratifiedGroupKFold` on the entity, since a customer's snapshots in two folds would let it recognise the customer rather than the targeting; and the snapshot date joins the `FEATURE_AFTER_TREATMENT` candidates, because a snapshot taken after the treatment date already contains the campaign's effect. `uplift_validation.json` gains optional `treated_rows`, `control_rows`, `entity_column`, `treated_entities`, `control_entities` (null in older reports), which the Data page shows. *Consequences:* the code is documented in `docs/UPLIFT.md` §3 and `docs/DATA_CONTRACT.md` §11, and `tests/unit/uplift/test_uplift_two_column_keys.py` fails if either document misses a code of `UPLIFT_VALIDATION_CODES`.
- **DEC-855 — The uplift hold-out is grouped by entity.** *Context:* `split_holdout` drew rows stratified on `(t, y)`; with snapshots, a customer's March row could be trained on and their April row graded, so the hold-out would measure memory. *Decision:* `split_holdout(..., groups=)` draws whole entities, stratified on the entity's arm (constant, by DEC-854) and whether the entity converted at any snapshot, with the same seed and the same half-up rounding per cell. The train flow passes the entity column for a composite key, whatever `split.type` the use case configures (an uplift split is always random-by-customer), and `split.json` records `group_column`. Without `groups` the function is byte-for-byte what it was. *Consequences:* no customer is on both sides (tested at unit and end-to-end level); there is still no validation part — an uplift learner is fitted without one — so "train / validation / holdout grouped by entity" reduces to train / hold-out.
- **DEC-856 — Uplift models store Phase 1's drift baseline; feature drift is Phase 1's PSI.** *Context:* uplift runs stored no drift baseline and scoring runs wrote no drift, a documented limit (UPLIFT.md §15). *Decision:* the uplift train flow's register stage writes `drift_baseline.json` through `engine.stages.register.drift_baseline`, over the **raw** training columns the feature spec kept (the same frame `schema.json` is written from), and sets `ModelVersion.drift_baseline_key`. A scoring run passes the raw uploaded frame and that baseline to `engine.stages.score.compute_drift` — no PSI code is duplicated. Raw on both sides is the right pairing for uplift, because the feature spec is replayed on raw scoring columns rather than through Phase 1's prepare. A model trained before M53 has no baseline: feature drift is then `null` with the reason, never an invented zero (DEC-051). Phase 4b's scheduled drift check replays `prepare.json`, which an uplift model does not have, and already reports "not measured" for it; that is unchanged. *Consequences:* `scoring_summary.json` of an uplift run now carries `drift_status`/`drift_max_psi`/`drift_summary` like a Phase 1 run's, and the manifest records `drift_max_psi`.
- **DEC-857 — The treated-share check: an absolute tolerance, `uplift.drift_treated_share_tolerance: 0.05`.** *Context:* an uplift model's segments and expected conversions were measured on a campaign with a particular treated share; new data from the same experiment with a very different share is a different design. *Decision:* when a scoring file carries the model's treatment column with 0/1 values, `uplift_drift.json` compares its treated share with the model card's `propensity`: `within_tolerance` when `|current − training| ≤ uplift.drift_treated_share_tolerance` (default 0.05, range 0–0.5, not agent-editable, overridable per run — it cannot make data look causal), else `outside_tolerance` (logged at WARNING). An absolute difference rather than a two-proportion z-test, because a test's verdict scales with the file: on a million rows it calls a 0.2-point move significant, on two hundred it misses a 10-point one. The pooled two-proportion p-value (`engine.uplift.incrementality.two_proportion_p_value`, reused) is recorded beside it for information. A file without the column, with a non-0/1 value or with no rows is `not_applicable` with the reason — the usual scoring file carries no treatment, because its campaign has not happened yet. *Consequences:* a new artefact `uplift_drift.json` (`UpliftDriftReport`, in `UPLIFT_ARTEFACTS`) is written by every uplift scoring run: Phase 1's `DriftReport` for the features (or null and why), the `TreatmentShareDrift`, and a one-line summary shown on the Running screen and the Data/Output pages.
- **DEC-858 — Phase 1's pages render uplift runs; `GET /runs/{id}/artefacts/{name}` whitelists `UPLIFT_ARTEFACTS`.** *Context:* a scoring run of an uplift model is a Phase 1 run (DEC-651) and opened in Phase 1's Data, Model and Output pages, which requested `prepare.json`, `drift.json` and `decile_lift.json` (404s in the console) and printed propensity wording on uplift numbers; the artefact route answered `ARTEFACT_UNKNOWN` for every uplift artefact. *Decision:* `read_artefact` adds `or name in UPLIFT_ARTEFACTS` — still a whitelist, never a path join; `GET /runs/{id}/uplift/{name}` stays as an alias. `ui/pages.js` keeps `PAGE_ARTEFACTS` for Phase 1 and adds `UPLIFT_PAGE_ARTEFACTS` and `pageArtefacts(kind, run)`, chosen by `run.problem_type === "uplift"`; `app.js` requests that list. For an uplift run: **Data** shows rows, treated and control (in customers and rows for a two-column key), the treated share, the randomness check against its limit (and whether a failure was acknowledged), causal yes/no, rows set aside, the train/test split with its group column, every uplift finding, and on a scoring run the drift card; **Model** shows AUUC and the Qini coefficient with their intervals, the ATE and uplift at 10/20/30%, the hold-out arms, and the Qini curve — drawn by the uplift module's own `qiniChart`, which `app.js` loads with a dynamic `import()` only for an uplift Model page (so `pages.js` keeps its static import graph inside Phase 1's modules and the two screens draw one picture; without the module the page lists the curve's points); **Output** shows the KPI the engine wrote, the four segments, the targeting recommendation, drift and the treat list under the run's key label. The Previous runs list labels every run's problem type (`train · Uplift`), and the Results strip uses the run's own problem type rather than the use case's. *Consequences:* no Phase 1 page requests an artefact an uplift run never writes (pinned by `tests/unit/uplift/test_phase1_pages_uplift.py` and its node suite); a Phase 1 run renders exactly as before.
- **DEC-859 — `POST /runs` refuses an uplift training run with `422 UPLIFT_REQUIRES_UPLIFT_ROUTE`.** *Context:* the catalog hides uplift from Phase 1's Setup (DEC-608), but a direct API caller could train uplift through `POST /runs`, where the uplift checks run only inside the job and fail late as `RUN_BLOCKED_BY_VALIDATION`. *Decision:* one synchronous check after the configuration is resolved: a **training** run whose resolved problem type is uplift — by override (`path: overrides.problem_type`) or by the use case's own configuration (`path: null`) — is refused, pointing to `POST /uplift/runs`. Scoring an uplift model stays on `POST /runs`: a scoring file needs no treatment column and is checked against the model's schema. The route's policy row is unchanged (Analyst), and a denied-role test covers it. *Consequences:* the refusal arrives before a run exists, in words that say where to go.
- **DEC-860 — The principal-hash salt is a secret with no default in code (ruling R3).** *Context:* every principal hash (consent ledger, erasure register, audit `principal_hash`) was salted with `settings.client_id`, else `DEFAULT_SALT = "local"` (DEC-705, DEC-733). The client id is written on every artefact, so anyone holding a hash and a customer list could test ids against it. *Decision:* `Settings.privacy_salt` (`MARKETING_AI_PRIVACY_SALT`) is a `SecretStr`, in `SECRET_FIELDS`, of at least 16 characters once stripped (`MIN_SALT_LENGTH`), with no default, and `DEFAULT_SALT` is gone. `engine.privacy.config.privacy_salt(settings, engine=...)` returns the setting when it is set. Without it: on `env=prod` it raises `SettingsError SETTING_REQUIRED`. When the platform database is a local SQLite file it generates `secrets.token_urlsafe(32)` once, writes it beside that file as `privacy_salt` (mode 0600, `O_EXCL`, so the first writer of a race wins) with one WARNING, and reads it from there afterwards; a file shorter than 16 characters is `SETTING_INVALID`. Anything else (Postgres on `dev`/`staging`, an in-memory database) is `SETTING_REQUIRED`, because there is no file to keep a generated salt in. `install_privacy_checks` (a `PHASE_APP_HOOKS` entry) calls `check_privacy_salt`: `create_app(settings)` raises at once on prod without the salt, and the deployed `api.main:app` checks at startup, so uvicorn stops before serving anything. This differs on purpose from sign-in (DEC-702), because every hash written without the secret would have to be thrown away. The privacy routes and `consent_gate_for_run` pass the platform or ledger engine, so the salt stays with the ledger it protects. `DatabaseStack` creates `PrivacySalt` (`marketing-ai/<env>/privacy-salt`, 48 characters, no punctuation, the deployment's key, `RemovalPolicy.RETAIN`), and passes it into the application secret as the `privacy_salt` key by dynamic reference, the same way the DSN reaches the task. Rotation is suppressed (`AwsSolutions-SMG4`) with the reason. *Consequences:* the salt is generated once, kept through `cdk destroy`, and never rotated: a new salt would leave every existing hash unmatchable, so an erased person's request could not be found again and a stored consent could not be checked. The same applies to salts written before M54: a local ledger or register hashed with the client id or `local` does not match the new salt, and `local` cannot be set back because it is shorter than 16 characters. No deployment has run Phase 4b yet (M50 is open), so only local data is affected. `infra/naming.SETTINGS_FIELDS` gains the field, and the infra snapshots gain the secret.
- **DEC-861 — Sign-in is throttled per account and per address, and a locked sign-in is refused before the password is checked.** *Context:* `POST /auth/login` answers every wrong password with the same 401 (DEC-720). That stops account enumeration but not unlimited guessing. *Decision:* `engine.access.throttle.LoginThrottle` counts failed sign-ins in a sliding window (`login_failure_window_seconds`, default 900) under two keys. One is the account: `account_key` is the typed username, stripped and case-folded, so an unknown username locks exactly like a known one and a lock-out confirms nothing. The other is the client address. Either key locks for `login_lockout_seconds` (900) when it reaches its limit: `login_max_failures_per_account` (5) or `login_max_failures_per_address` (20). While either is locked, `/auth/login` answers **429 `LOGIN_LOCKED`** with `Retry-After` in whole seconds and a message in minutes. The check comes before `check_credentials`, so the answer never reveals whether the password was right. The account is reported first when both are locked. A successful sign-in clears only its account's failures. The address's count is kept, so a sprayer that guesses one account does not reset its count against the others. When a lock expires, the count starts again. Each scope tracks at most 50,000 keys and drops the least recently failed first. `api.access.client_address` uses the peer address unless `trusted_proxy_hops` (`MARKETING_AI_TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS`, 0–5, default 0) is set. Then it takes the N-th `X-Forwarded-For` entry from the right; entries further left are ignored, and a request with fewer entries than N is counted by its peer. Audit: the failure that starts a lock-out carries the new `DETAIL_KEYS` entry `lockout` (`account`, `address` or `account,address`), and every attempt refused while locked is `denied` with `reason_code: LOGIN_LOCKED` and the `lockout` scope. The clock is injected, and nothing logs a username or an address. *Consequences:* state is in memory and per process, like `AnonymousEventLimiter` (DEC-724). With several workers the effective limit is multiplied by their number: the limit is weaker, but the wrong person is never locked out. A restart forgets every lock. The five limit settings join `infra/naming.SETTINGS_FIELDS`.
- **DEC-862 — Separation of duties: the person who trained a model cannot approve or promote it, and every decision is recorded with its reason.** *Context:* `approved_by` and `promoted_by` were names the caller typed (Phase 1 had no identity), and nothing tied an approval to the person who trained the model. *Decision:* `RunRecord.requested_by` is a new defaulted field, so every older `run.json` still reads. It is set from the request's principal by `POST /runs`, the uplift run route (`POST /uplift/runs`) and, for a scheduled or manually fired dataset run, `engine.scheduling.firing.start_dataset_run`, and to the firing principal's user id for a scheduled run. `engine.approvals.trainer_of` reads it from the training run. `separation_refusal` refuses a signed-in user (`kind == "user"`) whose id equals the trainer's. `POST /models/{id}/approve` and `/promote` answer **403 `SEPARATION_OF_DUTIES`** ("You started the training run that produced this model, so another Approver has to decide on it."), with that `reason_code` in the audit event. With sign-in on, the registry's `approved_by`/`promoted_by` is the signed-in username whatever the body says; with sign-in off it is still stored as typed. `POST /models/{id}/reject` (Approver, required `reason` of 3–2000 characters) archives a `pending_approval` version and answers **409 `INVALID_TRANSITION`** for any other status: a champion is replaced by promoting another model, never by rejecting it. The trainer may reject their own challenger. Every approval, rejection and promotion is appended to `model_decision` (decision, `decided_by` as a user id, reason, the champion at that moment, time). *Consequences:* with sign-in off there is one operator holding every role, so the rule cannot be enforced, and `GET /approvals` returns `separation_enforced: false` so the screen says so. A run recorded before `requested_by` existed has no known trainer, and the rule is not applied to it. A scheduler-trained challenger can be approved by any Approver. An app built without access control has no principal and records no decision rows.
- **DEC-863 — Erasure runs as a background job, retrying store by store, and can be retried.** *Context:* `POST /privacy/erasure` scanned and rewrote every artefact inside the request and answered 201 or 500 (DEC-752), which on a real store is minutes of an open HTTP request, and one unwritable file failed the whole request. *Decision:* the route writes the register row as `queued` (`queue_request`) under the id it mints, sets the audit details (`request_kind`, `principal_hash`, `client_id`, `trigger: queued`), hands the work to `ErasureJobs` and answers **202** `ErasureAccepted`, with `Location: /privacy/erasure/{id}` and a `progress_url`. `ErasureJobs` has one worker thread that takes `_STORE_REWRITE_LOCK`, the lock the retention routes take (DEC-749), so requests run in the order they were made. `erase` groups the findings by store and writes one `erasure_progress` row per store (files total, files done, attempts, status, `STORE_WRITE_FAILED`), plus a `consent_ledger` row when history is deleted. A file whose write raises `StorageError` or `OSError` is retried with the rest of its store's failures, up to `max_attempts` (3), waiting `default_backoff` between tries (2, 8, then 30 s). The other stores carry on meanwhile. A store still failing ends the request `failed` with `ERASURE_STORE_FAILED`, with its counts recorded and its models flagged. `GET /privacy/erasure/{id}/progress` (Admin) returns status and per-store progress only, with no hash, so polling it is a plain read. `GET /privacy/erasure/{id}` stays the audited completion report. `POST /privacy/erasure/{id}/retry` (Admin) needs the id in the body again, because it was never stored. The id is hashed and checked against the request's hash (**422 `PRINCIPAL_MISMATCH`**), and only a `failed` request can be retried (**409 `ERASURE_NOT_RETRYABLE`**). The retry re-opens the row (`resume=True`), re-finds whatever still holds the person, adds its counts and flagged models to what earlier attempts recorded (`merge`), and flags no model twice. The start is the audit event of the `POST` (or retry, `trigger: retry`). The job appends the end as `privacy.erasure.complete` by the requesting Admin under the same request id, with the counts or the failure code and `failed_stores` (new `DETAIL_KEYS`: `attempt`, `failed_stores`).
