A crash in the job is caught by `fail_if_unfinished`. *Consequences:* the id lives in the job's memory from the request until the job ends, no longer than the synchronous version held it. `ERASURE_RUNS_IN_PROGRESS` (DEC-728) no longer answers 409 on the `POST`: the request ends `failed` with that code and can be retried through the API once the run finishes. The jobs are per process, and a restart during a job leaves the row `queued` or `in_progress`.
- **DEC-864 — The head-to-head is what training recorded, on the challenger's test split, and nothing is re-scored or estimated.** *Context:* M54 asks the Approver to see both models on the same held-out data with both metrics, but the train flow kept only the champion's primary metric (`champion_<primary>`) from its re-score (DEC-044). *Decision:* `engine.pipeline` now adds every catalog metric of the champion's re-score on the challenger's test split to `run_manifest.json` as `champion_<metric>`. `engine.approvals.head_to_head` reads the challenger's values from its `evaluation.json` and the champion's from those manifest keys. For each metric it gives the challenger's value, the champion's value, the difference and which model the metric favours (honouring `greater_is_better`, with a tolerance of 1e-12 for equal), plus `rows_evaluated` and the champion rule's `improvement_pct`. When there is no `evaluation.json` (an uplift model) or it lacks the primary metric, the primary row comes from the registry's `test_score`. A metric with no recorded champion value is shown without one, and `note` says why: no champion when the model was trained; the re-score was not recorded; or older runs recorded only the headline metric. `stale` is true when the champion has changed since the comparison, and the note says approval will be refused (`CHAMPION_CHANGED`). `GET /approvals` (Viewer; optional `use_case`) lists every `pending_approval` version newest first, with its head-to-head, `trained_by`, earlier decisions, and `can_decide`/`blocked_reason` for the caller (the approve route's role refusal first, then separation of duties). *Consequences:* a decision screen never puts compute behind a click, and it can never disagree with the number the champion rule used. Runs trained before Plan D show the primary metric alone and say so.
- **DEC-865 — Migration 0005, and autogenerate sees the platform tables.** *Context:* the Plan D tables need a migration, and `alembic/env.py` limited autogenerate to `METADATA_TABLES`, so the Phase 4b tables were invisible to `make revision`. DEC-797 left that alone and filed a cross-branch request for both halves of the fix. *Decision:* `alembic/versions/0005_plan_d.py` (revises `0004`) creates `model_decision` (primary key `decision_id`, index on `model_id`) and `erasure_progress` (primary key `(request_id, store)`, counts and codes only, never the principal), with every timestamp `DateTime(timezone=True)` (DEC-339). `downgrade` drops both. `PLATFORM_TABLES` gains the two names. `env.py` imports every platform row class (users, sessions, audit, consent, erasure request and progress, retrain flags, schedules, firings, alerts, `ModelDecisionRow`) and then sets `include_name` to `INCLUDED_TABLES = METADATA_TABLES | PLATFORM_TABLES`, in that order, because a table the filter admits but the metadata lacks is one autogenerate proposes to drop. *Consequences:* this supersedes DEC-797 and closes the 2026-09-23 phase-4b-production → phase-4a-aws request in `docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md`. `test_plan_d_migration.py` runs `alembic check` against a database migrated to head. A later platform-table change can be autogenerated instead of written by hand.
- **DEC-866 — Peak memory is converted correctly on macOS, and the one-million-row run is recorded by a script (M57).** *Context:* Phase 1 plan §11 asks for 1M rows within the time limit on a laptop. Every one-million-row figure so far comes from a 4-CPU container, and `bench_large_file.py` divided `ru_maxrss` by 1024 as KiB, which on macOS (bytes) reports a peak 1,024 times too large. *Decision:* `peak_memory_mb` divides by 1024² when `sys.platform == "darwin"`. `scripts/bench_1m.py` runs `bench_large_file` at 1,000,000 CSV rows in a child process, parses the `measured:` and `peak memory:` lines it printed, names the machine (CPU model, usable CPUs, RAM, OS, Python), and with `--record` appends one row under the marker in `docs/PERFORMANCE.md`. `--kind` (`laptop`, `desktop`, `container`, `server`) is required, so a container run can never be recorded as the laptop run. *Consequences:* nothing in the row is estimated. The laptop row is an owner action, and until one is recorded plan §11's laptop criterion is stated as **not met**.
- **DEC-867 — Login throttle hardening: the ALB is one trusted hop, one attempt per account at a time, eviction never lifts a lock, an Admin reset lifts it.** *Context:* the M54 review found that behind the deployed ALB `client_address()` returned the ALB's private IP for everyone (`trusted_proxy_hops` defaulted to 0 and infra never set it), so 20 bad logins locked sign-in for all users; that check-then-act in `POST /auth/login` let parallel guesses exceed the per-account limit; that memory-bound eviction could drop locked entries; and that the 429 suggested a password reset that did not clear the lock. *Decision:* `infra/compute.py` publishes `trusted_proxy_hops=1` (`ALB_PROXY_HOPS`; the tasks' security group admits only the ALB, which appends to `X-Forwarded-For`), and `client_address` joins every `X-Forwarded-For` header line before splitting; `LoginThrottle.attempt(account)` holds a per-account-key mutex (a map bounded by attempts in flight) around the lock check, the credential check and the recording; eviction drops the oldest unlocked entry and falls back to the oldest only when all are locked; `LoginThrottle.clear(account)` runs on an Admin password reset and when a user is re-enabled, and an address lock's 429 no longer mentions a reset. *Consequences:* one client can no longer lock everyone out; N parallel wrong guesses get at most `max_failures_per_account` 401s; addresses are not serialised, so a parallel spray can overshoot the address limit by the requests in flight; when every tracked key is locked, a new key's failures are forgotten until space frees; the infra snapshots gain one SSM parameter.
- **DEC-868 — Every Settings field has a row in the deployment field table, enforced by a test.** *Context:* the five login-throttle settings and `privacy_salt` shipped without rows in `docs/AWS_DEPLOYMENT.md` §3.2, and `docs/PRODUCTION.md` still said there was no login rate limiting. *Decision:* add those rows in the existing format (`privacy_salt` shown as arriving in the application secret from the database stack's `PrivacySalt` secret; `trusted_proxy_hops` shown as written `1` by `cdk deploy`), document the throttle in `docs/PRODUCTION.md` §1.1, and add `tests/unit/test_docs_honesty.py` tests that require a `| \`field\` | \`VARIABLE\` |` row for every `engine.settings.ENV_VARS` entry and a description of the throttle in `PRODUCTION.md`. *Consequences:* a new `Settings` field fails the unit suite until its deployment row exists; a variable only mentioned in passing does not count as documented.
- **DEC-869 — Rejecting re-checks the status under the registry's lock.** *Context:* the M54 review found that `POST /models/{id}/reject` read `pending_approval` outside the registry's lock and then archived, so an approval landing between the read and the write made the challenger champion and the reject archived it, leaving the use case with no champion. *Decision:* `ModelRegistry.archive` (the protocol, `SqlRegistryStore` and `engine/aws/s3_registry.py`, which passes it through) takes an optional `expected_status`; the store compares it inside its lock and session and raises `INVALID_TRANSITION` when it differs. The reject route archives with `expected_status=pending_approval` and maps the refusal to `409 INVALID_TRANSITION`. The champion rule (`should_promote`) is untouched. *Consequences:* a reject that loses the race answers 409 and the champion stays; every other caller of `archive` behaves as before.
- **DEC-870 — The full future-data leak check exists beside the narrowed one, as `full_leak_check`, and the build report says which ran.** *Context:* Ruling R1 accepted DEC-096's narrowed leak probe (only the snapshot rows of the entities given future-dated events, plus up to 500 entities given none) as the default, on three conditions: a `full_leak_check: true` option; the full check forced on for the first build of any new recipe (DEC-871); and the full check run nightly and in the golden tests (DEC-873). *Decision:* "Full check" means the probe as it was before DEC-096 in *which rows it rebuilds*: every snapshot row, compared against the build's features as a whole frame. It still stacks the future rows inside DuckDB (DEC-096's `UNION ALL` view), so it never copies an event table. `build._run_leak_probe(full=True)` does this; `_leak_probe` keeps its signature and verdict for its existing callers. `engine.onboarding.build.build_dataset` gains `full_leak_check` and `first_build_of_recipe` (both default false). `POST /datasets` gains `full_leak_check: bool = false` in `DatasetBuildRequest`. The option belongs to the build request, not to `OnboardingSpec`: it changes how hard a build is checked, not what the dataset is, and a field on the spec would have changed every stored `spec_hash`. `BuildReport` gains `leak_check: LeakCheckRecord | null` with `scope` (`full` or `narrow`), `reason` (`first_build_of_recipe`, `option` or `default`), `recipe_hash`, `rows_total` (snapshot rows built), `rows_probed` (snapshot rows the probe rebuilt) and `summary` (one engine-worded sentence). It is null only when the build stopped before the probe ran, or for a report written before M55. When a build is both a recipe's first and asked for the full check, the reason is `first_build_of_recipe`, the rule R1 is checked against. The build logs the scope, reason, row counts and probe seconds at INFO. The SQL guard (`features.point_in_time_join_clause` and `assert_point_in_time` on every compiled query) is unchanged. So are FUTURE_EVENTS_LEAKED's severity, its non-acknowledgeability, its row counts and DEC-109's float tolerance. *Consequences:* the full check costs one more feature aggregation over the whole spine at the validate stage. That was DEC-096's 10.7 profiled seconds at 20,000 customers before the copy was removed. The measured cost is in docs/PERFORMANCE.md §8. `scripts/bench_onboarding.py --full-leak-check` measures it at any size.
- **DEC-871 — "The same recipe" is `recipe_hash`: the logic that decides what a feature reads, without ids or times. The full check is forced when no earlier successful build of it is registered for the client.** *Context:* R1 forces the full check on "the first build of any new onboarding recipe". The repo records a recipe as an `OnboardingSpec` (with a `spec_hash`) plus its `MappingSpec`s, and each passed build as a `DatasetManifest` registered in the client store (`ClientStore.register_dataset`/`list_datasets`). `spec_hash` hashes the spec's source and mapping ids. A monthly replay (`engine.onboarding.replay`) and a scheduled rebuild (`engine.scheduling.firing.latest_recipe_inputs`) change those ids every month without changing any logic, so `spec_hash` would have made every month a "new recipe". *Decision:* `engine.onboarding.specs.recipe_hash(spec, mappings)` is a sha256, with its own domain separator, over: the use case; the feature spec; the label spec; the snapshot rule; and, for every mapping, its role and its column decisions (client column, standard column, transform). Mappings are sorted, so their order does not count. It leaves out every id (spec, mapping, source, client, dataset), every timestamp, a mapping column's `confidence` and `decided_by`, a mapping's `unmapped_source` and `missing_required`, and `value_maps`, which repeats what the transforms say. The label is included, so editing only the churn definition makes a new recipe. That is conservative: it costs one more full check. A replay that had to drop a column the old mapping read is a new recipe. `DatasetManifest` gains `recipe_hash: str | null`, which is null for datasets built before M55. `build.is_first_build_of_recipe(spec, mappings, earlier)` is true unless `earlier` holds a manifest of the **same client** with the **same `recipe_hash`**. The callers that register datasets pass `store.list_datasets(client_id, use_case)` and forward the answer: `POST /datasets`, where it is decided when the request is accepted; the preview; and `build_dataset_from_spec` in `engine/scheduling/firing.py`, a three-line change to a Phase 4b file. Only a build that passed is registered, so a failed build, a cancelled build, a preview (never registered) and a pre-M55 dataset (no `recipe_hash`) do not count. Another client's build of the same recipe does not count either: the rule is about this client's data. *Consequences:* a client's first build of a recipe, and the first build after any change to its logic, is full. Monthly replays and scheduled rebuilds of an unchanged recipe are narrow. The first build of an existing recipe after M55 runs the full check once. Two builds of a new recipe requested before either has registered both run full. `build_dataset` itself has no client store. A caller that does not pass `first_build_of_recipe` (the benchmark, tests, a future caller) gets the narrowed default, so a new production entry point must call `is_first_build_of_recipe`. The report's `default` sentence says only that the full check was not asked for and is required only on a recipe's first build, so it is true for those callers too.
- **DEC-872 — Why R1 keeps the full check: a join-key bug that moves one entity from another's events passes the SQL guard and the narrowed check, and only the full check reports it.** *Context:* DEC-096's consequences name its blind spot: a leak that moves only entities that received no future rows and are outside the 500 control entities. R1 asked for a test that builds such a leak through the real machinery. *Decision:* `tests/unit/onboarding/test_leak_check.py` and `tests/integration/test_onboarding_leak_check.py` use the smallest honest seam. They replace `features.point_in_time_join_clause`, the one function that writes the join, with an edit a person could plausibly make: "an account's own events, or its linked account's" (the next id), written `e.entity_key = s.entity_key AND e.event_time <= s.snapshot_date -- GUARD OR e.entity_key = s.entity_key + 1`. AND binds before OR, so the linked branch has no time bound. The guard line is still exactly what the real function writes, so the unchanged `assert_point_in_time` accepts every compiled query, and a test asserts that it does. No guard is disabled. The fixture puts all 500 of the probe's future rows on the last customer. Its "linked" reader is the customer before it: not injected, and outside the first 500 non-injected customers the narrowed probe takes as controls. The end-to-end build on real CSVs, real mappings and the whole `build_dataset` shows three things. The narrowed check reports nothing, the build **passes and writes a dataset** whose victim row counts another customer's whole history. The full check (`full_leak_check=True`) reports FUTURE_EVENTS_LEAKED as an unacknowledgeable error and writes nothing. So does a first build (`first_build_of_recipe=True`) without the option. *Consequences:* the narrowed check's blind spot is pinned by a test that fails if the premise changes. If a later change makes the narrowed check see this bug, the test says the DEC-096/R1 trade-off must be re-read. At real scale with date-sorted files, the probe's injected rows land on hundreds of entities, so a uniform off-by-one key bug would probably move some probed entity and be caught by the narrowed check by chance. A bug confined to a few linked pairs would not. That is the case the full check on first builds, and nightly, is for.
- **DEC-873 — The full check runs nightly on the M37 benchmark tables (one tenth of the M14 size) and in the golden tests.** *Context:* R1 wants the full check nightly and in the golden tests. The nightly workflow runs `make test-all`, which includes `@slow`. The full-size fixture is the M14 target: 200,000 customers, 5,000,000 usage rows, 12 snapshots, 60 features and 1.4 GB of CSVs. On this repository's 4-CPU, 15.7 GiB container it builds in about 276 s plus the full check, peaks at about 9.3 GB, and needs its tables generated first (docs/PERFORMANCE.md §6). That is too much for a standard GitHub-hosted runner inside the nightly job's 60-minute budget, which the slow training suite already uses a quarter of, and a build the runner kills for memory is not a check that runs. *Decision:* `tests/integration/test_onboarding_leak_check.py::test_the_full_check_passes_on_the_benchmark_tables` is `@slow`. It generates the benchmark tables at the M37 size (20,000 customers, 500,000 usage rows), builds the benchmark's own recipe with `full_leak_check=True` (detected roles, suggested mappings, the first 60 suggested features, 12 snapshots), and asserts four things: the build passed, `leak_check` is full, `rows_probed == rows_total`, and nothing leaked. Its docstring says why it is not the full size and how to run that by hand (`scripts.bench_onboarding --customers 200000 --usage-rows 5000000 --full-leak-check`). The golden tests run the full check too. `test_onboarding_flow.py` (M13's end-to-end build, which `test_onboarding_build_reads.py` reuses) now builds with `full_leak_check=True` by default and asserts that the report and manifest record it. `test_features.py`'s hand-computed golden fixture is put through the build's probe with every row rebuilt. The browser acceptance journey (`test_onboarding_acceptance.py`) asserts that its first build, a new client's new recipe, showed the full check and the first-build reason. *Consequences:* the nightly full check covers 240,000 snapshot rows, not 2,400,000. The full size stays a manual measurement, to be recorded in docs/PERFORMANCE.md §8 when it is run. At the M37 size under a load average of about 12, the full probe took 18.6 s against the narrow probe's 1.0 s, adding 11 s to a 102 s build, with no higher peak memory and the same dataset fingerprint. Running the benchmark generator for this test exposed a hang. `scripts/bench_onboarding.write_sources` runs in a spawned child. The fingerprint render pool (`engine.stages.ingest`) is shut down only by `atexit`, which a multiprocessing child never runs, and the child's exit joins the idle pool workers, which wait indefinitely. So the child never exited once a table exceeded one 100,000-row chunk. The generator now joins the pool (`shutdown(wait=True)`; `wait=False` was tried and was not enough). The same hazard applies to any other code that fingerprints in a multiprocessing child. That is an engine-level follow-up (for example a `multiprocessing.util.Finalize` on the pool), not made here.
- **DEC-874 — The build review shows which leak check ran.** *Context:* R1 wants the build report to record whether the full or the narrow check ran. *Decision:* the Build step's review (`ui/modules/onboarding/steps.js`) adds one card, "Future-data check", showing `leak_check.summary`, the engine's own sentence with the measured row counts, for example "Full future-data check: all 240,000 snapshot rows were rebuilt with events dated after the last snapshot added, because this is the first build of this recipe for this client." The card carries `data-leak-check="full|narrow"`. A report without `leak_check` shows no card. `tests/unit/test_onboarding_ui.py` pins `BuildReport.leak_check` and `LeakCheckRecord.scope`/`summary` as fields the panel reads. No control to ask for the full check was added to the screen. The first build of a recipe already gets it, and the option is on the API (`POST /datasets`), which keeps the UI change small. *Consequences:* a reviewer can see, per dataset, whether it was built under the full check and why. `GET /datasets/{id}/report` returns the same record.
- **DEC-875 — The prototype harness waits for the page to settle, not for the first re-render or a fixed time.** *Context:* two uplift tests failed about one run in three on a busy machine (`docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md`, 2026-09-23 integration → phase-3b-uplift). Root cause, confirmed: `go()` sets `location.hash` and renders synchronously, and jsdom *also* queues a `hashchange` (a zero-delay `window.setTimeout` in its session history) whose handler, `render()`, replaces `#app main` a second time. `upload()` returned at the first replacement of `<main>`. On a quiet machine the file read (two `setImmediate` hops in jsdom's FileReader) ended first; under load the already-due hashchange timer fired first, so `upload()` returned after the hashchange re-render with the file still unread: `#f-ptype` was not drawn yet ("DEC-608 … never offers Uplift"), and `#f-run` was still disabled ("an uploaded file's uplift Output …", via `scoreUpload` after `go(dom, UP)`). A 20-line jsdom script reproduced it deterministically (a `go()` then an upload returns with `#f-ptype` null; 50 ms later it is drawn). Fixing only that exposed two more guesses of the same kind under the same load: `submit(); await wait(1300)` asserting a run that had not finished yet ("Campaign results for a run with a 0% control group…": `nothing to click: #f-again`), and `click(link); await wait(30)` where jsdom follows the link in one timer hop and fires `hashchange` in a second, so a late first hop let the 30 ms wait resolve in between. *Decision:* `tests/prototype/harness.mjs` counts the page's asynchronous work - every file read (through `Blob.prototype.text`, which is the only way the prototype reads a file) and every page timer (through `window.setTimeout`/`clearTimeout`, which jsdom's own hashchange also uses) - and `settle(dom)` resolves when none is left, one macrotask after the last one ended so the re-render it set off in microtasks has run. A leading zero-delay barrier covers jsdom's one untracked hop (link following on Node's clock). `upload()` now awaits `settle()` and keeps its check that `<main>` was replaced; every `await wait(N)` in the prototype suites (33 in uplift, 4 in onboarding, 3 in use cases) became `await settle(dom)`, and `wait` was removed from the harness so a fixed sleep is not reintroduced. No assertion changed. `marketing-ai-prototype.html` did not change, so the screenshots and the byte-level consistency checks are untouched and `CHANGELOG-prototype.md` needs no entry. *Consequences:* the suites wait exactly as long as the page takes, so they are also faster on a quiet machine (89 tests: ~65-96 s → ~50 s). A page change that adds a recurring timer would make `settle()` time out (10 s) with a message naming the pending timers, rather than hang.
- **DEC-876 — Flakiness is measured under load with a repeatable loop.** *Context:* "passes three times on my machine" is how these tests shipped. *Decision:* `scripts/loop_prototype_tests.sh COUNT TEST_FILE` runs one suite COUNT times and prints the pass count; `LOOP_LOAD=N` starts N busy loops for the length of the measurement and kills them on exit, failing runs' TAP is kept in `LOOP_LOG_DIR`. *Consequences:* the before/after numbers (README.md, Plan D, M56) can be retaken by anyone with the same command.
- **DEC-877 — The jsdom and node suites run in CI, and a missing node or jsdom is a failure there.** *Context:* five pytest modules drive node (`test_production_ui_js`, `test_production_ops_ui_js`, `test_inactive_settings_ui`, `test_ui_journey`, `tests/unit/uplift/test_uplift_ui`) and skipped when node or jsdom was absent - which on CI was always, because CI never set up node; the prototype suite was not in CI at all. *Decision:* `tests/fixtures/node.py` holds the one rule (`skip_without_node()`, `skip_without_jsdom(dir)`), mirroring `tests/fixtures/postgres.py`: skip with a reason locally, fail under `REQUIRE_JSDOM=1`. The variable has no `MARKETING_AI_` prefix on purpose: it describes a test run, not a deployment, so `engine/settings.py`'s `NON_FIELD_ENV_VARS` and `infra/naming.py` stay untouched. `ci.yml`'s `lint-test` job sets `REQUIRE_JSDOM: "1"`, sets up node 22 (`actions/setup-node@v4`, `cache: npm` keyed on `tests/**/package-lock.json`), runs `npm ci` in every directory under `tests/` that has a lockfile (found, not listed), and runs `make prototype-test`, whose recipe now uses `npm ci` instead of `npm install` (a lockfile out of step with `package.json` fails instead of being rewritten). `tests/unit/test_container_files.py` pins all of it, including that every `package.json` under `tests/` has a lockfile beside it (both do: `tests/prototype`, `tests/integration/production/ui`, whose `ops/` subdirectory shares it; `tests/unit/uplift` needs node only). *Consequences:* a runner where node or `npm ci` went missing goes red, not green with a note.
- **DEC-878 — CI reads the fast suite's JUnit report for skips of must-run tests.** *Context:* the two `REQUIRE_*` variables only cover skips their fixtures know about, and nothing noticed a must-run suite that was deselected or renamed. *Decision:* `scripts/check_no_skips.py --junit REPORT --id REGEX…` fails (exit 1) when a test whose `classname::name` matches a pattern was skipped, naming it and its reason, or when a pattern matched no test that ran; exit 2 for an unreadable report. CI runs it after `make check-readme` on the same `junit-fast.xml`, with patterns for the five node/jsdom modules and the Postgres tests (`[postgres]` params, `test_postgres_metadata`, the Alembic and audit-table Postgres tests). `tests/unit/test_check_no_skips.py` covers it. *Consequences:* a new skip path, or a must-run test that silently leaves the selection, fails the build; skips outside the patterns (the playwright journeys, docker) are not its business.
- **DEC-879 — The Postgres tests already run in CI's `make test`; verified, not changed.** *Context:* M56 asked to confirm that CI's Postgres service is actually used. *Decision:* no `make test-postgres` step: `make test` is `-m "not slow and not bedrock and not aws"`, which selects the 42 `postgres`-marked tests, and `tests/fixtures/postgres.py`'s `skip_without_postgres()` calls `pytest.fail` under `MARKETING_AI_REQUIRE_POSTGRES=1`, which the job sets. Verified locally against a real PostgreSQL 16 on `localhost:55432`: 42 passed, 0 skipped; against an unreachable port with the variable set, each test fails naming the URL and the driver's error. DEC-878's check adds the outside view. *Consequences:* none beyond DEC-878.
- **DEC-880 — Saving a labelled recipe syncs its retraining schedule at once.** *Context:* a recipe's `monitoring.retraining` schedule was created only at the next API start or by `POST /schedules/retraining/sync` (DEC-783), and Phase 4b filed a cross-branch request for the save to do it. *Decision:* `POST /clients/{id}/onboarding-specs` and its replay call `api.routes.schedules.sync_recipe_retraining` after `save_spec`. It runs the same code as `POST /schedules/retraining/sync` (`sync_retraining_schedules` over `retraining_targets`), restricted to that recipe's client and use case. It does nothing for a recipe without a label, or when `scheduler_backend` is `none` (DEC-782). It is best-effort: a failure is logged by class name and the save still succeeds. *Consequences:* a labelled recipe's schedule exists as soon as the recipe is saved, and saving one recipe changes no other client's or use case's schedules. This closes the Phase 4b cross-branch request (DEC-783); a failed sync is repaired by the next start or by the sync route.
- **DEC-881 — A background erasure is claimed in one statement and a restart does not leave it running forever.** *Context:* erasure jobs live only in the API process's memory (DEC-863), so a request left 'queued' or 'in_progress' when the process stopped stayed that way and could never be retried. The retry route also answered 202 while the row still read 'failed', so the UI stopped polling at once and a double submit queued two jobs. *Decision:* on startup, `install_privacy_checks` runs `fail_interrupted`, which marks every 'queued'/'in_progress' row 'failed' with `ERASURE_INTERRUPTED`. This is safe only because a deployment runs one API process (DEC-861's premise). It skips a SQLite `platform.db` that does not exist yet and logs rather than aborts when the database cannot be reached. The retry route claims the request with one conditional UPDATE ('failed' to 'queued', clearing `error_code` and `completed_at`), answers 409 `ERASURE_NOT_RETRYABLE` when no row changed, and only then submits the job. A job moves only a 'queued' row of the same principal to 'in_progress'. *Consequences:* an interrupted erasure can be retried; progress reads 'queued' from the moment of the 202; two retries at once start one job; running more than one API process would now mark the other process's running jobs as failed.
- **DEC-882 — A retry does what the request asked: the history choice is stored and models are flagged before any rewrite.** *Context:* a retry inferred `history_all_clients` from `client_id`, which by then holds the deployment's id, so a request that named no client lost "every client's consent history" on retry. Models were flagged only after all rewrites, so a first attempt that failed midway flagged nothing, and the retry could no longer find the person in the stores already erased. The retry's report also left out exposure-unknown models flagged by the earlier attempt. *Decision:* migration 0005 (not yet deployed) adds `erasure_request.history_all_clients BOOLEAN NOT NULL DEFAULT false`, the same column is on the model, and `create_privacy_tables` adds it to older SQLite files with a guarded `ALTER TABLE`. `queue_request` records the choice and the retry reads it. `_erase` flags models right after `find_principal` and the busy-runs check and writes them to the request row at once. The outcome's `models_flagged` and `models_exposure_unknown` come from every flag recorded under the request id, and the status follows from them. *Consequences:* a request that fails midway has already flagged its models; a successful retry still reports `completed_with_exceptions` for models nobody could check; the existing test that expected no flags after `ERASURE_INCOMPLETE` now expects them; `ErasureRequestRecord` gains a defaulted `history_all_clients` field.
- **DEC-883 — The privacy salt is fingerprinted, and a different salt is refused.** *Context:* switching `MARKETING_AI_PRIVACY_SALT` (away from Phase 4b's client id, or to a different secret) left the API working while every stored consent and erasure hash silently stopped matching. *Decision:* a key/value table `platform_setting(key PK, value, updated_at timestamptz)` is added in migration 0005, `PLATFORM_TABLES` and `alembic/env.py`. The first time `privacy_salt` is used with an engine, it stores sha256("marketing-ai/privacy-salt-fingerprint\0" + salt), never the salt. A later mismatch raises `SettingsError` `SETTING_INVALID` naming `MARKETING_AI_PRIVACY_SALT` (503 from a route). If no fingerprint exists but `consent_record` or `erasure_request` already has rows, a WARNING says those hashes were made with the old salt (the client id) and must be re-imported, and the fingerprint is then recorded. SQLite creates the table itself; a Postgres database not yet migrated to 0005 is not checked. *Consequences:* a salt change fails loudly instead of orphaning data; changing the salt on purpose means re-importing the hashed data and deleting the fingerprint row; the fingerprint reveals nothing about any person and cannot be reversed to the salt.
- **DEC-885 — Only Plan D's own plan text could be committed under `docs/plans/`.** *Context:* Plan D M58 asks for the Phase 2, 3a and 4a plans, Plan B (uplift), Phase 4b, the Phase 5 design and Plans D, E and F to be committed under `docs/plans/`. *Decision:* only Plan D's text was available to the branch, and it is committed unchanged. The others Plan D M58 lists (Phase 2, 3a, 4a, Plan B, the Phase 5 design, Plans E and F) are listed as missing in `docs/plans/README.md` rather than reconstructed: a plan rewritten after the event would read as the source of decisions it was written to justify. Phase 4b's plan stays at `docs/PHASE4B_PLAN.md`, the path `PARALLEL_WORK_PROTOCOL.md` §4 cites. *Consequences:* the missing plans are re-filed as an owner action in `docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md`; each is to be added unchanged when the owner supplies it.
- **DEC-887 — Dataset-run tests record the training job instead of running it.** *Context:* the tests in `tests/integration/test_runs_from_dataset.py` that `POST /runs` successfully submitted a real training job to the app's thread pool, which nothing cancelled, so the pytest process kept running after its summary until the training finished (a Phase 3b cross-branch request). *Decision:* the module's apps that accept a run get `tests/unit/production/scheduling_support.RecordingJobs`, which records a submitted job without starting it (the `quiet` fixture already stubbed `build_job_fn` to a no-op and keeps doing so). The assertions read `run.json` and the refusals, which exist before any training starts, and are unchanged. *Consequences:* no training job outlives the suite. This closes that cross-branch request.
- **DEC-888 — `alembic` autogenerate sees the platform tables.** *Context:* `alembic/env.py` and `tests/unit/production/test_plan_d_migration.py` cite this number for the change DEC-865 records. *Decision:* as DEC-865: `env.py` imports every platform row class, then `include_name` admits `METADATA_TABLES | PLATFORM_TABLES`, in that order. *Consequences:* see DEC-865; a platform-table change can be autogenerated with `make revision`, and `test_plan_d_migration.py` runs `alembic check` against a database migrated to head.
- **DEC-889 — "Fire now" acts as the person who asked, so separation of duties holds for it.** *Context:* the M54 review found that `POST /schedules/{id}/fire` ran the firing as `SYSTEM_SCHEDULER`, so a challenger trained by a manual firing recorded the scheduler as `requested_by` and the person who clicked (holding Analyst and Approver) could approve their own model (DEC-862). *Decision:* the manual firer's `FiringServices.principal` is the request's principal, so the challenger's `run.json` names them and the approve route refuses them with `403 SEPARATION_OF_DUTIES`; the route's one audit event (written by the middleware) carries them as actor. Firings nobody started - the local scheduler, an EventBridge invocation - still act as `SYSTEM_SCHEDULER`. *Consequences:* the rule "whoever started the training cannot approve it" holds for every way a person can start a training run; a scheduled firing's challenger can be approved by any Approver, since no person started it.

<!-- ---- PHASE-2 (onboarding) — append only below this line ---- -->

## Phase 2 — Data onboarding (DEC-100 … DEC-199)

- **DEC-100 — The onboarding *vocabulary* lives in `engine/config.py`'s PHASE-2 block; `engine/onboarding/specs.py` holds the *artefacts* and re-exports both.** *Context:* the Phase 2 plan §3 says "all pydantic models in `engine/onboarding/specs.py`", but four of them — the standard schema, the feature definition, the label and the snapshot definition — are blocks of a use-case YAML and of `engine.yaml:defaults`, so `UseCaseConfig` has fields of their types. `engine/config.py` imports nothing of ours by design and `engine/contracts.py` imports it one-way; defining the vocabulary in `specs.py` would mean `config → specs → contracts → config`, a cycle. *Decision:* the vocabulary a YAML file can contain (`StandardSchemaConfig`, `FeatureDef`, `SubAggregation`, `WhereClause`, `LabelDefinition`, `SnapshotDefinition`, `OnboardingConfig`, `RoleCatalogue` and their enums) is defined inside this branch's `PARALLEL_WORK_PROTOCOL.md` §4 block at the foot of `engine/config.py`, which is what it is — configuration vocabulary; the saved, hashed, per-client documents (`SourceProfile`, `MappingSpec`, `FeatureSpec`, `OnboardingSpec`, `DatasetManifest`, `BuildReport`, `BuildStatus`) are defined in `engine/onboarding/specs.py`; and `specs.py` re-exports the vocabulary, so the plan's promise holds at the import site. `LabelSpec` and `SnapshotSpec` are aliases of the config models rather than copies, so the configured default and the saved spec can never drift apart. *Consequences:* the dependency graph stays one-way and acyclic; the block is append-only and rebases cleanly; nobody has to know which half a model lives in to import it.
- **DEC-101 — The build report carries `OnboardingCheck`, a twin of `ValidationCheck`, rather than widening the Phase 1 model.** *Context:* plan §7 gives the onboarding checks the same contract as the Phase 1 ones and then re-runs the full Phase 1 validation on the assembled dataset, appending its findings to the same list — so one type has to accept both code tables. The obvious change is to `ValidationCheck._known_code` in `engine/contracts.py`, which sits above this branch's block, and `PARALLEL_WORK_PROTOCOL.md` §4 forbids editing above the block. *Decision:* `OnboardingCheck` is defined in `engine/onboarding/specs.py` with the same five fields plus `source_id`, its own code table (`ONBOARDING_VALIDATION_CODES`, the 26 codes of plan §7, which never overlap the Phase 1 19), and `from_validation_check()` to carry a Phase 1 finding across unchanged. It also refuses `acknowledgeable=True` on `FUTURE_EVENTS_LEAKED`, because plan §7 says that code is a bug and must never be user-acknowledgeable, and a model is a better place to enforce that than a convention. `docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md` asks for the two models to be unified in a later reviewed change on `main`. *Consequences:* the Phase 1 contracts file is untouched by this branch, so `test_contracts.py`'s 19-code assertion and `test_shared_file_markers.py` both hold; the cost is one duplicated field list, recorded here so it is closed deliberately rather than forgotten.
- **DEC-102 — A dataset directory has its own artefact registry, separate from the run one.** *Context:* `engine.contracts.ARTEFACT_REGISTRY` is pinned name-for-name against Phase 1 plan §7's run-directory list by `tests/unit/test_artefact_registry.py`, whose whole point is that a new artefact is added deliberately rather than absorbed silently. `dataset_manifest.json`, `build_report.json` and `build_status.json` are not run artefacts: they live in `data/datasets/<dataset_id>/`, they outlive any single run, and several runs can reference one of them. *Decision:* `DATASET_ARTEFACT_REGISTRY` and `DATASET_ARTEFACTS` live in `engine/onboarding/specs.py` and cover the dataset directory; `ARTEFACT_REGISTRY` stays exactly the run contract it has always been, and a test asserts the two never overlap. *Consequences:* the Phase 1 registry test passes unedited and keeps its meaning; "what does a run write?" and "what does a build write?" stay two answerable questions rather than one list of everything.
- **DEC-103 — `configs/roles.yaml` is engine data, like `engine.yaml:catalog`.** *Context:* plan §4.1 gives the role catalogue as a config file, and principle 7 says the engine executes specs and never contains client-specific logic. A role is not a per-use-case setting: `bills` means the same thing for every client, and a use case that could rename or remap a role would be a use case that could branch the engine by YAML. *Decision:* `roles.yaml` is loaded by `load_role_catalogue()`, cached per root like `engine.yaml`, never merged into a use case and never overridable. Exactly one role has `kind: entity` and every event role requires the same two standard columns, `entity_key` and `event_time`; both are validated on load, and a missing file is an error rather than an empty catalogue, because "this client has no roles" and "the engine lost its role table" are different problems and only one is the user's. *Consequences:* adding a role is a YAML block; `RoleCatalogue.require()` answers an unknown role by listing the ones that exist.
- **DEC-104 — The onboarding cross-field checks live in `engine/onboarding/roles.py`, not in `UseCaseConfig._cross_field`.** *Context:* `engine/config.py` validates each Phase 2 block on its own, but the checks that read two blocks at once — a suggested feature must not be named after a standard column, a forward-looking label must name an *event* role — need `UseCaseConfig._cross_field`, which is above this branch's §4 block. *Decision:* those checks are `validate_onboarding_config(config, roles)` in `engine/onboarding/roles.py`, a file this branch owns outright, and `tests/unit/onboarding/test_roles.py` runs them over every shipped use case so CI catches in seconds what the config loader would otherwise have caught on load. The cost is the moment of failure: a use case naming a role that does not exist fails when onboarding reads it rather than when the loader does. *Consequences:* `engine/config.py` never imports a role catalogue it has no other use for, and this branch rebases that shared file without conflicts; the parametrised test is the thing that keeps the trade honest, so it must never be skipped.
- **DEC-105 — Specs are hashed by one function: sorted keys, no timestamps, no ids.** *Context:* plan §13 rule 15 requires two identical specs to hash identically, or the dataset lineage and the future Phase 5 archive are worthless. A naive `model_dump_json()` hash includes `created_at` and `spec_id`, so the same recipe saved twice would be two recipes. *Decision:* `spec_hash()` in `engine/onboarding/specs.py` is the only hasher; it dumps in JSON mode with sorted keys and separators, excludes `VOLATILE_FIELDS` (`created_at`, `built_at`, `hash`, `spec_id`, `mapping_id`, `dataset_id`, `fingerprint`), and prefixes a domain separator so a spec hash can never collide with a file or dataset hash. `MappingSpec.with_hash()` and `OnboardingSpec.with_hash()` are the only writers of a `hash` field. *Consequences:* a recipe re-saved a day later is recognisably the same recipe; a changed window is recognisably a different one; the property is pinned by a test that saves the same recipe under two ids and two timestamps.
- **DEC-106 — `UseCaseConfig` gains four optional fields above this branch's block, announced rather than worked around.** *Context:* `PARALLEL_WORK_PROTOCOL.md` §3 assigns the `standard_schema`, `suggested_features` and `label` sections of `configs/use_cases/*.yaml` to Phase 2, and §4 forbids this branch from editing `engine/config.py` above its block. Those two rules contradict each other: `_Base` sets `extra="forbid"`, so a use-case YAML cannot carry a section the model has no field for, and the contracts-first task could not add the fields because the Phase 2 plan was not in the repository when it ran (`docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md`, 2026-09-22). *Decision:* the four field declarations are added, and they are the *only* edit this branch makes above its block in that file. All four are optional with defaults, so every existing config validates unchanged and neither Phase 3a nor Phase 4a is affected; their annotations are forward references to types defined in the PHASE-2 block, resolved by a `model_rebuild()` at the foot of that block; and every cross-field rule that would have needed more room above the block went to `engine/onboarding/roles.py` instead (DEC-104). The change is announced in `docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md` the day it lands, as §3 requires. *Consequences:* the other two branches rebase over four defaulted fields, which cannot change their behaviour; had this been done the other way — a separate `configs/onboarding/*.yaml` — the plan's own §5.1 and §5.2 examples would have been wrong and the protocol's ownership table would have pointed at sections that do not exist.

- **DEC-107 — A built dataset is resolved into the shape the upload path already consumes; a periodic one is refused by name.** *Context:* plan §6.5 change 4 has `POST /runs` take an `upload_id` or a `dataset_id`. The two differ in provenance, not in what a run needs from them: a storage key holding one row per entity, a profile of it, and a row count. *Decision:* `_DatasetSource` reduces a dataset to exactly those three, so the whole validate-create-submit path below is the one Phase 1 already had — the difference between Phase 2 reaching the engine and Phase 2 forking it. `run.json` gains `dataset_id`, `client_id` and `dataset_fingerprint` (four optional fields above this branch's §4 block in `engine/contracts.py`, and `upload_id` widened to nullable, announced in `docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md` as DEC-106 was), because without them the lineage the build recorded stops at the dataset and never reaches the model trained on it. Four refusals, because these are four different problems with four different answers: `DATASET_NOT_FOUND` (404), `DATASET_USE_CASE_MISMATCH`, `DATASET_CLIENT_MISMATCH` and `DATASET_NOT_BUILT` (409), and `DATASET_COMPOSITE_KEY_NOT_WIRED` (501). `client_id` without a `dataset_id` is refused too: it was accepted and ignored, which is how a caller comes to believe they scoped a run to a client when they scoped it to nothing. *Consequences:* the periodic path is the honest stop — plan §6.5 change 1 widened the key on the contracts, and teaching `prepare`, `split`, `explain`, `actions` and `export` to carry both columns is the half still outstanding; a run that took the first column would join on the customer, lose the snapshot date, train one row per customer out of twelve and report success.
- **DEC-108 — A build reads every row; the profiling row cap is lifted explicitly.** *Context:* `engine.stages.ingest.read_upload` defaults `row_cap` to `DEFAULT_PROFILE_ROW_CAP` (2,000,000), and `FileSourceReader.read` went through `read_table`, which does not pass one. For a *profile* that default is right and deliberate — a profile of two million rows describes a file as well as a profile of ten million, and `DatasetProfile.row_count` stays exact either way (DEC-046). For a *build* it is silently wrong: the rows are aggregated, so a capped read produces feature values computed from part of the history, for a subset of the entities, with nothing anywhere saying so. Measured at the plan's target size: every event table came back exactly 2,000,000 rows long and the build failed seven checks it should have passed. *Decision:* `FileSourceReader.read` calls `read_upload` with an explicit `row_cap=UNBOUNDED_ROWS`, and a test asserts it does. The cap is lifted deliberately rather than inherited by accident, and the reason is written where the call is. *Consequences:* a build of a very large source is bounded by memory rather than by a silent cap, which is the correct trade — a build that cannot fit should fail loudly, not quietly produce a wrong dataset. Nothing below two million rows changes, which is why no test caught this.
- **DEC-109 — The leak probe compares floats to a tolerance and everything else exactly.** *Context:* `_leak_probe` re-runs the feature spec against tables carrying rows dated after the last snapshot and reports any feature whose value moved. It compared with `Series.equals`, which is exact. DuckDB aggregates in parallel, so the order it sums a column in is not fixed between runs: building the same features twice over *identical, untouched* tables returns floats that differ in their last bits. Measured at 240,000 rows over 3,000,000 events, the largest such difference was 5.5e-12 on a sum of order 500 — about 1e-15 relative, which is arithmetic noise. So every float feature was reported as moved as soon as a build was large enough for DuckDB to use a second thread, and the finding it raised was `FUTURE_EVENTS_LEAKED` — the one a user may never acknowledge (`OnboardingCheck` refuses to mark it acknowledgeable, by design). Large builds were impossible, and the engine's own correctness check was what made them so. *Decision:* counts, dates and categories are still compared exactly, because a count that changes at all has counted something it should not have; a value that appears or disappears is a change whatever its type, so null positions are compared exactly in both branches; floats are compared at `rtol=1e-9`, a thousand times the measured noise and many orders of magnitude below any real leak, since an event that should not have been counted moves a count by a whole 1 and a mean by a real amount, never by a part in a billion. *Consequences:* the probe still fails the build when the time bound is deliberately broken, which the flow test asserts; a test pins the non-determinism itself, so the tolerance can be removed if DuckDB ever becomes order-stable — but only with a measurement showing it.
- **DEC-110 — `complaints.text` is documented as unredacted rather than the detector changed.** *Context:* `configs/roles.yaml` promised that PII in the free-text complaint column "is redacted at profiling time". It is not. `engine.stages.ingest.detect_pii` and `engine.stages.prepare._detect_pii` both match a whole cell (`pattern.fullmatch`), so a phone number that *is* the cell is caught and one buried in a sentence is not — measured on a synthetic complaints table at 363 of 1,105 rows carrying an email the shipped pattern finds by `search`, with `detect_pii` returning nothing for the column. That is defensible for the columns Phase 1 sees, where a `phone` column holds phone numbers, and exactly wrong for the free-text column Phase 2 introduces, which is where a customer types "call me back on 0400 123 456" and which Phase 3 will read to write summaries. *Decision:* correct the claim, not the code. Switching to a substring match would start redacting columns that are not redacted today in *every existing use case*, changing what Phase 1 trains on; `min_value_match_rate` and `min_distinct_ratio` are calibrated for whole-cell matching and would need recalibrating with them. That is a decision for the human reviewer, and it is filed in `docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md`. The role description now says the detectors match whole cells and the column should be treated as unredacted free text, and `tests/fixtures/raw/make_raw.py` plants contacts in the exact shapes the shipped patterns recognise so whoever takes the decision has something to test against. *Consequences:* a documented gap rather than an undocumented one; the promise the config made is the kind of claim that is worse than silence, because a reader plans around it.
