## Phase 4b — Production: access, audit, privacy, scheduling (DEC-700 … DEC-799)

Part 1 of `docs/PHASE4B_PLAN.md` (M46–M49), built with local backends and no AWS account. The
hundred was allocated by area so that entries written in parallel could not collide: foundation
700–709, access and audit 710–729, privacy 730–759 (engine 730–745, API 746–754, privacy and
monitoring screens 755–759), scheduling 760–789 (engine 760–774, API 780–786), sign-in and admin
screens 790–794, integration 795–799. Numbers inside a sub-range that the entries skip are unused.

**Legal note.** The DPDP entries (DEC-730 … DEC-759) are engineering controls that support
compliance. They are not legal advice; Minfy's legal or compliance team must review the final
design. Known dates: the Digital Personal Data Protection Rules were notified on 13 Nov 2025, with
compliance required by 13 May 2027 and penalties up to ₹250 crore.

### Foundation (DEC-700 … DEC-706)

- **DEC-700 — `PHASE_APP_HOOKS` in `create_app`.** *Context:* access control and the audit trail must apply to every route, including Phase 1's and the other phases' routers that Phase 4b may not edit; neither can be expressed as a router, and a dependency added after a router is included does not reach that router's routes. *Decision:* `api/main.py` gains `PHASE_APP_HOOKS: list[Callable[[FastAPI], None]]`, called with the new app **before** any router is included; it is the one edit above a block in that file. Phase 4b registers `install_access` (a global dependency plus the audit middleware) and `install_scheduling` (startup and shutdown handlers) in its block. *Consequences:* every route, `/healthz` included, inherits the enforcement dependency without its router knowing; the list is empty on a checkout without Phase 4b, so nothing changes there.
- **DEC-701 — `Settings` gains a Phase 4b section whose defaults change nothing.** *Context:* sign-in, audit export, the scheduler and alerts each need configuration, and the protocol forbids changing an existing field. *Decision:* added fields only — `auth_mode`, `auth_session_ttl_seconds`, `audit_export_bucket`, `audit_export_prefix`, `audit_retention_days`, `scheduler_backend`, `scheduler_tick_seconds`, `scheduler_group_name`, `scheduler_target_arn`, `scheduler_role_arn`, `alert_backend`, `alert_sns_topic_arn` — each with a `MARKETING_AI_*` variable in `ENV_VARS`, and `eventbridge`/`sns` added to `_REQUIRED_FOR`. Every default reproduces the behaviour before Phase 4b: nobody signs in, nothing fires on its own, an alert is a log line, an audit export goes to local disk. *Consequences:* a Phase 1–4a deployment that upgrades behaves exactly as before until it opts in.
- **DEC-702 — `auth_mode` is `off` or `local`; `off` acts as a named principal; prod fails closed.** *Context:* a laptop needs no sign-in, a deployment must have one, and the identity provider (decision P5) is not chosen. *Decision:* `off` makes every request act as `LOCAL_OPERATOR` (all four roles), so the audit trail still names an actor; `local` uses the built-in user store. `Settings` does **not** refuse `env=prod` with `auth_mode=off` — Phase 4a's prod contexts and tests predate the field — instead `api/access.py` answers every non-public route with 503 `AUTH_NOT_CONFIGURED` (naming `MARKETING_AI_AUTH_MODE`) while `/healthz` keeps answering, and logs an ERROR at startup; on dev or staging with sign-in off it logs a WARNING. OIDC/Cognito is deferred to M50 pending P5. *Consequences:* a misconfigured prod deployment stays up and says why, rather than failing to boot or serving unauthenticated. *Amended by DEC-725 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-703 — Roles are a set, not a ladder.** *Context:* the plan names Viewer, Analyst, Approver and Admin; a ladder would make an Admin an Approver. *Decision:* `Principal.roles` is a set; Viewer is implied by any role; Admin does **not** imply Approver or Analyst (separation of duties: the person who manages users cannot also approve the champion unless separately granted it). `SYSTEM_SCHEDULER` holds Analyst only. *Consequences:* scheduled work can score, check drift and train a challenger but can structurally never approve or promote; an Admin sees approve buttons refused with the reason. *Amended by DEC-723 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-704 — One policy table keyed by (method, path template); fail closed.** *Context:* "every route declares its required role", but the legacy routers belong to other workstreams. *Decision:* `api/access_policy.py` holds `RoutePolicy(role, action, object_type, object_param, audit_reads, purpose)` keyed by method and route template. Legacy routes are declared centrally (`LEGACY_POLICIES`); each Phase 4b router registers its own next to its routes at import. A route with no policy answers 403 `ROUTE_HAS_NO_POLICY`, and a test fails if any mounted route lacks one. Reads are audited only when they export row-level data (`audit_reads=True`). *Consequences:* a new route is refused until someone decides who may call it.
- **DEC-705 — No data values in the audit log.** *Context:* the audit trail must itself not become a copy of customer data (plan M47, the Phase 1 logging-audit approach). *Decision:* `AuditEvent.details` accepts only keys in the closed `DETAIL_KEYS` set, holding identifiers, codes and counts; a data principal appears only as `principal_hash(id, salt)`; before/after are hashes, never bodies. *Consequences:* adding a detail is a reviewed edit of `DETAIL_KEYS`; tests byte-search the audit table for planted values.
- **DEC-706 — `platform.db` beside `registry.db`; Alembic owns the Postgres schema.** *Context:* users, sessions, audit, consent, erasure and schedules need tables. *Decision:* locally a SQLite `platform.db` beside the artefacts (and `registry.db`); on a deployment the registry's Postgres. `create_tables(engine, names)` creates only the named tables and only on SQLite (DEC-340's scoped creation); the Postgres schema is Alembic's, in revisions `0002` → `0003` → `0004` after `0001`. *Consequences:* a Phase 1 data directory gains `platform.db` only when a Phase 4b feature is used.

### M46–M47 — Access control and audit (DEC-710 … DEC-722)

- **DEC-710 — Password hashing.** *Context:* the local user store needs passwords without adding a dependency. *Decision:* PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 from `hashlib`, a 16-byte random salt per user, `hmac.compare_digest`, stored as `pbkdf2_sha256$<iter>$<salt>$<hash>` with 600,000 iterations (OWASP 2023). Unknown usernames are checked against a decoy hash so response time does not reveal which accounts exist. *Consequences:* the stored iteration count lets the default be raised later without breaking old hashes; tests pass a low iteration count through the constructor.
- **DEC-711 — Bearer sessions stored as a hash.** *Context:* a copied database must not let anyone sign in. *Decision:* the token is `secrets.token_urlsafe(32)`; only its unsalted SHA-256 is stored, which is safe because the token has 256 bits of entropy. Expiry comes from `auth_session_ttl_seconds`. Logout revokes one session; disabling a user, changing their roles or changing their password revokes all of their sessions. *Consequences:* removing a role takes effect immediately; a password change signs the user out everywhere.
- **DEC-712 — The last Admin cannot be removed.** *Context:* a deployment with no Admin cannot be managed through the API. *Decision:* disabling or demoting the last enabled Admin is refused with 409 `LAST_ADMIN`, checked under a process lock inside one transaction; `scripts/create_user.py` is the recovery path. *Consequences:* two API processes on one Postgres could still race this rule; accepted, because it guards against a mistake, not an attack.
- **DEC-713 — Identity seam for M50.** *Context:* the identity provider (P5) is undecided. *Decision:* an `IdentityProvider` protocol with `DisabledIdentity` and `LocalIdentity`; the future OIDC/Cognito provider is a third class selected by a new `auth_mode` value. *Consequences:* the policy table, audit and UI read only the `Principal`, so nothing else changes when M50 lands; JWT validation and claim-to-role mapping are left to P5.
- **DEC-714 — Append-only below the application.** *Context:* the `AuditLog` protocol has no update or delete, but that only stops careless code. *Decision:* database triggers reject UPDATE and DELETE on `audit_events` (a plpgsql trigger that also rejects TRUNCATE on Postgres, via `0002`; `RAISE(ABORT)` triggers on SQLite, created by both the migration and `SqlAuditLog`). *Consequences:* changing history needs someone to drop a trigger first; the Object Lock export (DEC-715) covers the database owner. The Postgres trigger, TRUNCATE included, is pinned by a `postgres`-marked test run against a local PostgreSQL 16 at integration.
- **DEC-715 — Audit exports use S3 Object Lock in COMPLIANCE mode.** *Context:* governance mode can be bypassed by anyone holding `s3:BypassGovernanceRetention`. *Decision:* S3 exports are written in COMPLIANCE mode with RetainUntilDate = now + `audit_retention_days` and `ContentMD5`, behind the `AuditExportSink` protocol; local mode writes JSON lines to `<data_dir>/audit/exports`, never overwriting. The export's own audit event carries the file's SHA-256 as `after_hash` and its key as `export_key`. *Consequences:* a bucket without Object Lock fails the export loudly (502 `AUDIT_EXPORT_FAILED`); exports are copies, and the table remains the record. *Amended by DEC-726, DEC-727 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-716 — Legacy policy mapping.** *Context:* every route that existed before Phase 4b needs a role. *Decision:* GETs are Viewer; creating or changing work, and generative actions that spend tokens, are Analyst; approving or promoting a champion and approving copy are Approver only; AWS connection writes and test are Admin; row-level downloads (`runs.scores_download`, `runs.artefact_download`, `copy.messages_download`, `datasets.sample`) are `audit_reads`; each policy has a `purpose` phrase from which the refusal sentence is built ("Only an Approver can approve a champion."). *Consequences:* an Admin cannot approve (DEC-703); a test fails if any legacy write is open to a Viewer or has no purpose.
- **DEC-717 — Enforcement is a fail-closed global dependency.** *Context:* the policy is keyed by route template, which is only known after routing. *Decision:* a global dependency on `app.router.dependencies` (DEC-700); a missing policy is 403; `auth_mode=off` on prod is 503 per request (DEC-702); HEAD uses the GET policy. *Consequences:* FastAPI parses the body before dependencies run, so a malformed JSON body gets its 422 before the sign-in check (nothing runs). The OpenAPI, docs and static `/ui` routes are not APIRoutes and stay public.
- **DEC-718 — A pure-ASGI audit middleware writes one event per request.** *Context:* exactly one event per mutating or `audit_reads` request, with no buffering of downloads. *Decision:* the outcome comes from the status (401/403 `denied`; other 4xx/5xx and exceptions `failed`). `after_hash` is the SHA-256 of the exact 2xx JSON response bytes, hashed chunk by chunk as they pass. The request id is a uuid4, or an incoming `X-Request-ID` matching `[A-Za-z0-9._-]{8,64}`. A path parameter becomes `object_id` only if it matches `[A-Za-z0-9._:-]{1,128}`, so an email typed into a URL is withheld. Unmatched routes are recorded as `request.unmatched` with no path. *Consequences:* streaming is unaffected; routes enrich the event through `set_audit_context` (which may also override the action, outcome and actor) and never write a second one. *Amended by DEC-724 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-719 — A failed audit write does not fail the request.** *Context:* the audit database must not become a single point of failure, and the response has already been sent when the event is written. *Decision:* log at ERROR with the action and the exception class only, and increment `app.state.audit_write_failures`. *Consequences:* a deployment should alarm on that ERROR line; the gap is visible rather than silent.
- **DEC-720 — Login privacy.** *Context:* a sign-in form should not confirm which accounts exist or are disabled. *Decision:* wrong password, unknown user and disabled user all get the same 401 and message; the audit event is `failed` with `reason_code`, and `object_id` is set only when the user is known. The username and password are never recorded; a successful login's `after_hash` is the principal view, not the body that contains the token. *Consequences:* there is no rate limiting or lockout yet; only the PBKDF2 cost slows guessing.
- **DEC-721 — One list of Phase 4b tables.** *Context:* several migration tests need a definite table set, and they asserted "exactly one migration". *Decision:* `engine.platform_db.PLATFORM_TABLES` lists every table a Phase 4b migration creates; the migration tests assert one linear Alembic chain from `0001` and `METADATA_TABLES ∪ PLATFORM_TABLES`. *Consequences:* each Phase 4b migration appends its tables there; drift for Phase 4b tables is checked by Phase 4b's own migration tests, because `alembic/env.py` limits autogenerate to `METADATA_TABLES` (DEC-797).
- **DEC-722 — No password on the command line.** *Context:* argv is visible in `ps`, shell history and some supervisors. *Decision:* `scripts/create_user.py` reads the password from two hidden prompts or from the first line of stdin with `--password-stdin`, and records the creation as `system:bootstrap`. *Consequences:* automation pipes the password in; even the first Admin has an audit entry.

### M48 — DPDP controls, engine (DEC-730 … DEC-745)

- **DEC-730 — Privacy policy lives in `configs/privacy.yaml`.** *Context:* purposes, the use-case-to-purpose mapping, which artefacts are row-level and the erasure mode are policy, and engine code may not name a use case. *Decision:* one engine-data YAML loaded through a frozen pydantic model; the loader rejects an undeclared purpose or a use case that does not exist, and the result is cached per config root. *Consequences:* legal can review and change policy without touching code. An unmapped use case is never gated. A missing file means no controls; a file that is present but wrong raises.
- **DEC-731 — Latest consent record wins.** *Context:* consent changes over time. *Decision:* the ledger is append-only. For a client, principal and purpose, the latest record with `recorded_at <= at` decides, ties to the higher `seq`; consent is valid only if that record is an unexpired grant; no record means no consent. *Consequences:* history is preserved, and validity can be answered as of any past time.
- **DEC-732 — The scoring seam.** *Context:* stages cannot be edited. *Decision:* inside the PHASE-4B block of `engine/pipeline.py`, `_ScoreFlow._actions` is rebound. If a ledger exists for the run's client and purpose (client = `RunRecord.client_id`, falling back to `Settings.client_id`), the ledger's verdict is written into the configured consent column, or into a synthetic column that is never exported; the existing `consent_false` rule suppresses those rows and its counts report them, and `consent_report.json` is written and listed in `run.json`. With no ledger, the Phase 1 method runs. *Consequences:* a test proves the output is byte-identical to Phase 1 and that no `platform.db` is created. Training is not gated. If the ledger database is unreachable, the stage fails closed. *Amended by DEC-738, DEC-739 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-733 — Principal ids are hashed everywhere in the database.** *Context:* the ledger and the erasure register would otherwise be a copy of the customer list. *Decision:* `consent_record`, `erasure_request` and the audit log store only `principal_hash(id, salt=privacy_salt(settings))` (the salt is `client_id`, else `"local"`); lookups hash their input. *Consequences:* erasure never needs to touch the database, and the sentinel test can byte-search `platform.db`. Limitation: the salt is not secret, so ids with little entropy can be brute-forced; a secret salt in Settings is recommended.
- **DEC-734 — Consent CSV import is all or nothing.** *Context:* a consent file is personal data. *Decision:* every row is validated first; errors report row number and column and never the value; nothing is written if any row fails unless `partial=True`. Unknown columns are ignored and listed; timestamps more than 5 minutes in the future are refused. *Consequences:* the ledger never ends up half-loaded, and error messages are safe to show or log.
- **DEC-735 — One store map.** *Context:* retention, erasure and export all need to know where personal data lives. *Decision:* `engine/privacy/layout.py` maps each key prefix to a store and holds a `StoreIndex` that reads `run.json`, `upload.json` and dataset manifests as raw JSON for dates and primary keys. *Consequences:* the three features stay consistent, and documents from older schemas do not break a pass.
- **DEC-736 — Retention.** *Context:* `governance.retention_days` becomes enforced. *Decision:* the plan is the dry run and apply executes exactly the plan. Items are dated from their own records, never file times; anything undatable is skipped as `UNDATED`. A run uses the value in its `run_config.json`; an input (upload or dataset) uses the maximum of its use case's value and every reading run's; a source uses the maximum over the datasets built from it. `0` means "once a finished run has read it"; inputs of unfinished runs are skipped as `IN_USE`. Row-level files are deleted; samples in kept reports (`profile.json` columns and preview rows, `scoring_summary.json` sample rows) are emptied. *Consequences:* a test proves the dry run equals the real run and that models, manifests and aggregate reports survive. *Amended by DEC-744 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-740 — S3 lifecycle rules are only a backstop.** *Context:* a lifecycle rule knows prefix and age, nothing else. *Decision:* rules cover `uploads/`, `datasets/` and `clients/<id>/sources/` under `s3_prefix`; expiry is the longest `retention_days` plus `grace_days`, and noncurrent versions expire after `grace_days`. Only rules whose ID starts with the configured prefix are replaced; others are read back and kept; if the current configuration cannot be read, nothing is written. *Consequences:* row-level run artefacts are enforced only by the retention job; nothing applies the rules yet (an ops step). *Amended by DEC-708 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-741 — Erasure scans the whole store and rewrites in place.** *Context:* a fixed list of "known files" goes stale. *Decision:* read every artefact key. Tabular files (csv via the csv module, parquet via pyarrow) match on the primary key recorded for the file, or on any cell when none is recorded; JSON matches string values only; text matches exact tokens (`C-104` never matches inside `C-1042`). Each file is rewritten in its own format, LLM cache hits are deleted, binary files are reported rather than rewritten, and every rewritten file is scanned again (`ERASURE_INCOMPLETE` if the id remains). *Consequences:* a sentinel test proves no byte of the id is left anywhere and that the product still reads every rewritten file. *Amended by DEC-707, DEC-737 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-742 — Delete or tombstone.** *Context:* some clients need counts to reconcile after an erasure. *Decision:* `delete` is the default; `tombstone` keeps the rows, replaces the id with `[erased]`, empties other text cells and keeps numbers. Stray mentions are masked in both modes. The consent history is kept by default under the hash (`consent_history: keep|delete`). *Consequences:* the mode is a policy line in `privacy.yaml`, not code. *Amended by DEC-737 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-743 — Models are flagged, not retrained.** *Context:* plan M48 says retraining happens at the next scheduled cycle, not immediately. *Decision:* `model_retrain_flag` holds model versions whose training run read an upload, dataset or source (by lineage) that contained the person, or whose own run directory did. The scheduler reads `models_flagged_for_retraining`, retrains into a challenger (champion rule and approval still apply), then calls `clear_flag` (DEC-768). *Consequences:* no model fit inside an HTTP request, and no model crowned without approval. *Amended by DEC-709 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-745 — Access export.** *Context:* plan M48 item 4. *Decision:* `find_principal` is reused, and the export is one zip of CSV rows (each file's own header), JSON fragments with their JSONPath and text lines, plus consent and erasure history and a README. The zip is returned, never stored. *Consequences:* its completeness test mirrors the erasure test, and the export creates no new copy that a later erasure would have to find.

### M48 — DPDP controls, API (DEC-746 … DEC-754)

- **DEC-746 — A principal id goes only in a request body.** *Context:* an id in a path or query string reaches the uvicorn access log, load-balancer logs and browser history, none of which can be erased, and the audit middleware would turn a path parameter into `object_id`. *Decision:* every route that takes a principal id is a POST with a JSON body, including the two that only read (consent lookup, access request). The event's `object_id` is the request's own id (lookup id, `ar_…`, `er_…`) and the person appears only as `principal_hash`, salted with `privacy_salt(settings)`; no response echoes the id. *Consequences:* those two reads are audited as mutations (`audit_reads` is GET-only); a unit test fails if any privacy route declares a path or query parameter named like a principal.
- **DEC-747 — Privacy roles.** *Context:* the plan gives retention, users and settings to Admin. *Decision:* every M48 route is Admin — consent evidence, erasure and access exports are decisions made for the client organisation — except the per-run consent report, which is Viewer like every other report of a run (counts only). *Consequences:* an Analyst cannot erase or export a person; tests check both refusal and a Viewer reading the report.
- **DEC-748 — Retention apply re-plans and checks the hash.** *Context:* the dry run must equal the real run, and the server should not keep plans between requests. *Decision:* the dry run returns `plan_hash` over `planned_at` (normalised to UTC) and the items. Apply plans again as of the same `planned_at` and deletes only if the hash matches; otherwise 409 `RETENTION_PLAN_CHANGED` and nothing is touched. `as_of` may be in the past, never the future. *Consequences:* a run started meanwhile, a new file, or a second apply of the same plan are all refused; any process can apply a plan another planned.
- **DEC-749 — One store rewrite at a time.** *Context:* erasure and retention both rewrite artefacts. *Decision:* a process-wide `_STORE_REWRITE_LOCK` serialises them. *Consequences:* it does not coordinate with a running pipeline job or with other API processes. *Amended by DEC-728 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-750 — Where the consent report is served.** *Context:* `/runs/{run_id}/artefacts/{name}` is Phase 1's route, and `PRIVACY_RUN_ARTEFACTS` is kept apart from `ARTEFACT_REGISTRY`. *Decision:* `GET /privacy/runs/{run_id}/consent-report`; a run that was not gated answers 404 `CONSENT_REPORT_NOT_FOUND`, which the UI reads as "not gated". *Consequences:* `api/routes/runs.py` is not edited, and the generic artefact route still does not serve `consent_report.json`.
- **DEC-751 — Consent import and client resolution.** *Context:* a consent file is personal data and loads all or nothing (DEC-734). *Decision:* 201 with the report when rows were written; 422 with the same report when refused, so every problem shows at once. At most 64 MB, UTF-8, parsed off the event loop. The client is the request's `client_id`, else `Settings.client_id`, else 422 `CLIENT_ID_REQUIRED`, and must match `[A-Za-z0-9._:-]{1,128}`. *Consequences:* an empty file is also 422 with no rows imported.
- **DEC-752 — Erasure errors and one audit event.** *Context:* a failed erasure still needs an id, and the audit rule allows one event per request. *Decision:* the route mints the `er_…` id before calling the engine with `audit_log=None`, then enriches the middleware's event with `erasure_audit_details`. On failure it returns 500 with the engine's code, the message names the id, and the register row reads `failed`. *Consequences:* `GET /privacy/erasure/{id}` shows failures; a test counts exactly one `privacy.erasure` event.
- **DEC-753 — Access export audit.** *Context:* the trail must prove what was handed over. *Decision:* the zip is returned and never stored; the event records `after_hash` = SHA-256 of the zip, `object_id` = the export's `ar_…` id, and a count of files and stores. *Consequences:* a test shows no new file in the data directory holds the id afterwards.
- **DEC-754 — Retention uses the client store only if it already exists.** *Context:* using `ClientStoreDep` created an empty `clients.db` as a side effect of a dry run. *Decision:* use the store only when `app.state.client_store` is set or `<data_dir>/clients.db` exists, as `scripts/run_retention.py` does. *Consequences:* the dry run leaves the data directory byte for byte unchanged.

### M48–M49 — Privacy and monitoring screens (DEC-755 … DEC-759)

- **DEC-755 — Controls on the production module's own screens are refused as they are drawn.** *Context:* `gate.js`'s `ACTION_CONTROLS` table (DEC-792) exists for other workstreams' screens. *Decision:* the privacy and monitoring screens call `controls.actionButton(method, path)`, which asks `reasonFor` from `/auth/me` while painting; a refused route draws the control disabled, with the server's sentence beside it and as its title, and with the same `data-pb-gate` marker so the capture listener swallows clicks on it. *Consequences:* controls stay visible and explained, never hidden; when `/auth/me` is unknown nothing is refused; a static test fails if an `actionButton` names a route with no policy or a listed M48/M49 action loses its control.
- **DEC-756 — A data principal's id never leaves the request body (the UI side of DEC-746).** *Context:* URLs, the hash and browser history cannot be erased. *Decision:* the id is read from its input on submit and sent only in a POST JSON body; it is never kept in screen state, put in the hash or drawn again; answers show `principal_hash` only. The access-export zip is fetched through the wrapped fetch and saved under the server's file name. Each change shows its audit action and object id, with "Open in the audit log" for an Admin. *Consequences:* after a repaint the input is empty; a jsdom test fails if the id appears in any URL, the hash or the page.
- **DEC-757 — Destructive privacy actions need confirmation; retention applies only the plan on screen.** *Context:* erasure and retention cannot be undone. *Decision:* nothing is sent until the "I understand" or "I have reviewed" box is ticked. Apply sends back exactly the `plan_id`, `planned_at` and `plan_hash` of the dry run shown; afterwards (success or 409) the plan is marked applied or out of date and only "Plan again" leads to another apply. A refused consent file is shown as its report; `PRIVACY_NOT_CONFIGURED` is shown once in place of the forms. *Consequences:* a plan can never be applied twice from one screen; a Viewer's privacy screens make no privacy API calls.
- **DEC-758 — The monitoring screen family, and where a run's outcomes live.** *Context:* M49 reads are Viewer and changes are Analyst (DEC-780); Phase 1's Output page may not be edited. *Decision:* `#/monitoring/...` with tabs Schedules, Alerts, Missed runs and Outcomes; outcomes live at `#/monitoring/runs/<run_id>` (upload, performance against the alert level, incrementality input, consent report). Reports that do not exist yet are read as "not yet", not as errors. *Consequences:* an Admin without Analyst sees schedule changes refused (DEC-703); a slow answer never paints over a newer screen.
- **DEC-759 — What the schedule form sends.** *Context:* the payload must be exactly what the API validates. *Decision:* cadence Daily, Weekly, Monthly (02:00) or Custom, with the cron line sent only for Custom; timezone defaults to Asia/Kolkata; the client is omitted unless typed (the recipe's client is used, DEC-786), never `settings.client_id`; only typed parameter ids are sent; delete asks twice in the row; "Run now" shows the firing's own status; managed schedules show an explanation instead of the edit form. *Consequences:* jsdom tests assert the payloads byte for byte.

### M49 — Scheduling, monitoring and outcomes, engine (DEC-760 … DEC-774)

- **DEC-760 — Scheduling package and actor.** *Context:* M49 needs schedules, firing, monitoring and outcomes, and scheduled work must never be able to approve anything. *Decision:* everything lives in `engine/scheduling/` (engine-only, never imports `api`), and every firing acts as `SYSTEM_SCHEDULER` (Analyst only). *Consequences:* a firing can score, check drift and train a challenger, and structurally cannot approve or promote; the API routes are thin wrappers over `service.py`, `firing.py` and `outcomes.py`.
- **DEC-761 — Own five-field cron parser; portability checked when the schedule is written.** *Context:* EventBridge uses a six-field dialect with Sunday numbered 1 and rejects restricting both day-of-month and day-of-week. *Decision:* a small tested parser (numbers, `*`, lists, ranges, steps, 0/7 both Sunday, Vixie's day rule) with `next_after` computed in the schedule's timezone (default Asia/Kolkata), plus `to_eventbridge()`. Names, `L`, `W`, `#` and `?` are refused by name, and `CRON_NOT_PORTABLE` is raised everywhere. *Consequences:* a schedule that works on a laptop also works on EventBridge; no new dependency; presets are stored as their cron line.
- **DEC-762 — The local scheduler is a daemon thread and the table is its only state.** *Context:* development needs schedules to fire without AWS, and tests must be deterministic. *Decision:* `LocalScheduler` ticks every `scheduler_tick_seconds`, settles finished firings, then runs `run_due` for each enabled schedule; the clock is injectable, `tick()` is public, and one broken schedule never stops the others. *Consequences:* a restart loses nothing; `sync`/`remove` are no-ops.
- **DEC-763 — A due slot fires at most once.** *Context:* EventBridge retries, multiple replicas and sweeps can all reach the same slot. *Decision:* a unique index on `(schedule_id, scheduled_for)`; a firing inserts its row before doing any work, and the loser of the insert does nothing. Manual firings have a null slot, so "fire now" is always allowed. `next_due_at` moves on before the work starts. *Consequences:* at most one firing per slot even across processes.
- **DEC-764 — Missed runs and catch-up.** *Context:* slots can pass while nothing is running. *Decision:* a slot is on time if it is the only due slot and within the grace (2 ticks + 60 s locally, 30 minutes for the EventBridge sweep). Otherwise every due slot is recorded as a `missed` firing (at most 100), one `schedule_missed` alert is raised, and **one** catch-up firing runs, because every kind of work reads the latest data. The EventBridge sweep never claims a slot still inside the grace. Disabled periods produce no backlog. *Consequences:* missed slots stay visible in the history without repeating the same work. *Amended by DEC-775, DEC-779 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-765 — The EventBridge target is an ECS RunTask of this image running `scripts/fire_schedule.py`.** *Context:* the plan says "EventBridge Scheduler calling the API", but EventBridge Scheduler cannot call an arbitrary HTTPS endpoint without an API destination, which means a stored credential and an endpoint to defend. *Decision:* the target is the cluster (`scheduler_target_arn`); the task-definition family is the cluster name + `-job`; the network reuses `sagemaker_subnet_ids`/`sagemaker_security_group_ids`; the input overrides the container command with `--schedule-id <id> --scheduled-time <aws.scheduler.scheduled-time>`; retry is 2 attempts within 1 hour; `sync` uses Get then Create/Update (IAM grants no List). Create writes the table then pushes, rolling back the row if the push fails; delete removes the EventBridge schedule first. *Consequences:* no extra credential and the same `fire()` path everywhere; it matches `infra/operations.py`. The input substitution needs checking on the M50 dev deployment (moto cannot fire schedules).
- **DEC-766 — A firing uses the engine's own run path on the client's latest tables.** *Context:* a scheduled run must behave exactly like the API's. *Decision:* `start_dataset_run` repeats the dataset branch of `create_run_endpoint` call for call, with the same codes; scoring resolves the champion via `resolve_model_version`; datasets are built with `build_dataset` then `register_dataset`; `latest_recipe_inputs` swaps each recipe table for the newest source with the same confirmed role that contains every mapped column, reusing the saved mapping. *Consequences:* a scheduled run and a clicked run are indistinguishable; the route logic is duplicated in engine code and can drift (a follow-up can make the route call `start_dataset_run`).
- **DEC-767 — `monitoring.retraining` is live through managed schedules.** *Context:* the setting existed but nothing read it. *Decision:* `manual` → no managed schedule; `weekly`/`monthly` → one managed `retrain` schedule with that preset; `on_drift` → one managed weekly `drift_check` that retrains on drift. Managed ids are deterministic per client × use case × kind with `managed_by="monitoring.retraining"`; people's own schedules are never touched; managed schedules can be paused but not edited or deleted (`SCHEDULE_MANAGED`). The value is read from the use case's configuration, not from a run (DEC-795). *Consequences:* sync is idempotent and safe across replicas.
- **DEC-768 — Erasure flags are answered by the next retraining cycle; cleared only after a successful retrain.** *Context:* DEC-743. *Decision:* a `retrain` firing, or an `on_drift` drift check (which retrains when a model of its use case is flagged even without drift, result `RETRAIN_FOR_ERASURE`), records the flagged model ids in `flagged_models`; `settle` clears them only when the training run is `done` and registered a version. *Consequences:* the flagged champion stays champion until an Approver approves the challenger; under `manual` retraining flags stay open until a retrain schedule is fired by hand. *Amended by DEC-777 (Part 1 review).*
- **DEC-769 — One audit event per firing, ids only.** *Context:* M47 requires an audit trail without data values. *Decision:* every firing writes `schedules.fire` and every batch of missed slots writes `schedules.missed` (object type `schedule`, actor `system:scheduler`), with `firing_id`, `schedule_kind`, `dataset_id` (new `DETAIL_KEYS`), `run_id`, `trigger`, `outcome`, `reason_code`, `models_flagged` and `count`. A firing started through the API passes `audit_log=None`, so the middleware's event is the only one. *Consequences:* the audit trail joins to the firing history with no free text.
- **DEC-770 — Alerts are persisted first and written in business language.** *Context:* email is the least controlled output channel. *Decision:* every alert writes an `alert` row and one log line (kind and ids only); the SNS sink then publishes (subject ≤ 100 characters, ids in the body); a publish failure is logged by class name and never fails the job. Messages carry counts and ids, never values. Acknowledgement is recorded once and is idempotent. *Consequences:* alert history exists even with SNS down; there is no deduplication yet.
- **DEC-771 — Outcome window; the uploaded file is never stored.** *Context:* outcomes are incomplete until the label horizon has passed. *Decision:* the horizon is the recipe label's `horizon_days`, else the use case's `label.horizon_days`, else `OUTCOME_WINDOW_UNKNOWN`; the window starts at the dataset's last snapshot date when recorded, else at the run's `finished_at`; before `anchor + horizon` the request is refused with `OUTCOME_WINDOW_NOT_MATURED`, naming the date. The file is parsed in memory and never written. *Consequences:* retention and erasure gain no new row-level store; the artefacts hold aggregates only.
- **DEC-772 — Real-world metric: the model's own metric, on the control group when possible.** *Context:* treated rows were acted on, so a successful campaign would read as a model failure. *Decision:* use the version's metric at the threshold in `evaluation.json` (0.5 if missing), measured on control rows when there are ≥ 50 matched control rows (both classes for a classifier), otherwise on all matched rows; `metric_basis` says which. `relative_drop_pct` is signed by metric direction, and an alert is raised when it exceeds the run's `monitoring.performance_alert_drop_pct`. *Consequences:* honest performance alerts, with a documented basis.
- **DEC-773 — `IncrementalityInput` v1 is the interface Plan B consumes.** *Context:* Plan B is not in this repository. *Decision:* `runs/<id>/incrementality_input.json` is written only when the run held out a control group: treated vs control rows, suppressed rows excluded and counted, per group and band the rows, positives and outcome rate or mean, a descriptive `observed_difference` (no interval), the window, `control_group_fraction` and the unmatched count. `schema_version` changes only on a breaking change. *Consequences:* Plan B computes intervals and lift from aggregates alone.
- **DEC-774 — Migration `0004` and `OutcomeReport` v1.** *Context:* Postgres schemas belong to Alembic; Phase 5's Monitor will read outcome reports. *Decision:* `0004` creates exactly `schedule`, `schedule_firing` and `alert`, including the unique slot index; it is reversible, and a drift test compares it to the models. `OutcomeReport` fields are only ever added to. *Consequences:* `PLATFORM_TABLES` includes the three tables.

### M49 — Scheduling, monitoring and outcomes, API (DEC-780 … DEC-786)

- **DEC-780 — Who may use the M49 routes.** *Context:* the routes need roles consistent with DEC-716. *Decision:* every read (schedules, firing history, outcome reports, incrementality input, alerts, missed firings) is Viewer; creating, changing, pausing, deleting, firing and syncing schedules, uploading outcomes and acknowledging alerts are Analyst; nothing needs Approver or Admin. Starting scoring or training by hand is Analyst, and firings act as `SYSTEM_SCHEDULER`. *Consequences:* scheduling a retrain grants no right the Analyst did not already have; a test pins it.
- **DEC-781 — "Fire now" runs inside the request and is audited once.** *Context:* M47 allows one audit event per request, and the engine firer writes its own. *Decision:* `POST /schedules/{id}/fire` calls `ScheduleFirer.fire(trigger=manual)` in the worker thread with the services copied and `audit_log=None`, and passes `firing_audit_details` to `set_audit_context`. It answers 201 with the firing as recorded, which may be `failed` with an `error_code`. *Consequences:* the dataset build runs inside the request; the training or scoring run itself goes to the job runner; paused schedules can still be fired by hand.
- **DEC-782 — The scheduler starts and stops with the app.** *Context:* a thread must never start by default. *Decision:* `install_scheduling` adds startup and shutdown handlers. For `none` the startup does nothing at all; for `local` it syncs the managed schedules, then starts `LocalScheduler`; for `eventbridge` it only syncs. Create, update and delete push to the same cached `Scheduler`. *Consequences:* a startup failure is logged at ERROR and the API keeps serving; because nothing else settles API-started firings when the backend is not `local`, `GET /schedules/{id}/firings` settles running firings first.
- **DEC-783 — An on-demand sync route for managed retraining schedules.** *Context:* saving an onboarding recipe is Phase 2's route, so a new recipe's `monitoring.retraining` schedule would otherwise appear only at the next restart. *Decision:* `POST /schedules/retraining/sync` (Analyst) runs `sync_retraining_schedules`, idempotently, returning what it created, updated and removed; a scheduler failure is 502 `SCHEDULER_SYNC_FAILED`. *Consequences:* a Phase 2 follow-up could call the sync when a recipe is saved.
- **DEC-784 — Acknowledging an alert is Analyst work and happens once.** *Context:* acknowledging states that someone is dealing with it; a Viewer changes nothing. *Decision:* Analyst role; first acknowledgement wins; the audit event carries the alert's `before_hash` and ids only. *Consequences:* two people clicking together leave one name.
- **DEC-785 — The outcome upload stays in memory.** *Context:* the outcomes file is customer ids paired with what happened to them. *Decision:* multipart upload, format from the file-name suffix (`.csv`, `.parquet`, `.pq`; otherwise 415), refused over 256 MB before parsing, parsed off the event loop and never written; only aggregates are stored. The audit event carries run, use case, client, model, `count` = matched rows, `reason_code` = `PERFORMANCE_DROP` when an alert was raised, and `after_hash` of the report; never the file name. *Consequences:* a second upload replaces the report but each upload's hash stays in the trail; a test checks the customer ids appear nowhere in the audit trail, the platform database, the artefacts or any file name.
- **DEC-786 — A schedule's client defaults to its recipe's client.** *Context:* a recipe belongs to exactly one client, and firings compare the dataset's client with the schedule's. *Decision:* a given `client_id` must exist (404 `CLIENT_NOT_FOUND`); when omitted and `parameters.onboarding_spec_id` is set, the client comes from the recipe; it is never taken from `settings.client_id`, a different namespace (the cost tag). *Consequences:* a retrain or drift-check schedule without a recipe must name its client, or its firing fails with `NO_TRAINING_DATA`.

### M46–M47 — Sign-in and admin screens (DEC-790 … DEC-794)

- **DEC-790 — How the bearer token reaches every call.** *Context:* three request wrappers (`ui/api.js`, `onboarding/api.js`, `generative/api.js`) call the global `fetch` and cannot import each other's helpers; `app.js` makes its first call while loading. *Decision:* no wrapper is edited; `session.js` wraps `window.fetch` once, adding `Authorization: Bearer` to API-origin calls when a token is held and passing other origins through. The token lives in `sessionStorage`. The wrapper is installed by `boot.js`, a side-effect import from `router.js`'s PHASE-4B block, so it runs before `app.js`'s first render; `boot.js` must never import `router.js`, so the screens register from `index.html`'s block through `index.js`. *Consequences:* a later wrapper needs no change; with no token every call is byte for byte what the screen made; closing the tab signs out.
- **DEC-791 — Sign-in gate, status and return route.** *Context:* the UI needs to know when sign-in is required and who is signed in. *Decision:* any `401 AUTH_REQUIRED` except from `/auth/login` clears the token and goes to `#/signin/<encoded route>`; other 401/403s are left to their screen. `GET /auth/me` sets the status: 404 means no access control (the page stays as before Phase 4b), 503 `AUTH_NOT_CONFIGURED` puts the server's sentence in the bar. After sign-in the browser returns only to a hash inside the UI. Passwords are read on submit and never stored or re-rendered. *Consequences:* a revoked session (DEC-711) lands on the sign-in form; the screen that got the 401 briefly shows its own error first.
- **DEC-792 — Role-aware controls from one table.** *Context:* the controls live in other workstreams' screens, which this branch may not edit. *Decision:* `gate.js`'s `ACTION_CONTROLS` maps each existing control's selector to the route it calls, re-applied after every paint through a `MutationObserver` on `#app` and whenever the session changes. A refused control is disabled, not hidden, with the server's `reason` beside it and as its title; a capture-phase listener swallows clicks and submits on gated controls. When `/auth/me` is unknown or does not list a route, nothing is gated. *Consequences:* the server stays the enforcement; a static test fails when a row names a route with no policy, a gated `#id` is no longer drawn, or a required action is missing.
- **DEC-793 — Downloads that carry the sign-in.** *Context:* scores, campaign messages, templates and the audit CSV are plain `<a href>` links, which cannot carry a header. *Decision:* while a token is held, a capture-phase click on any API link fetches it through the wrapped fetch and saves the blob under the `Content-Disposition` name; a refusal is shown beside the link. With no token the browser follows the link as before. *Consequences:* audited reads are attributed to the real person; ctrl/meta/middle-click is not intercepted, so a new tab still gets 401.
- **DEC-794 — Admin screens.** *Context:* M46 asks for user management and M47 for an audit viewer with filters and CSV export. *Decision:* Users: list, add, change roles (checkboxes, with the hint "Admin does not include Approver or Analyst"), disable/enable, set password (your own asks for the current one); the server's `409 LAST_ADMIN` sentence is shown under the row. Audit viewer: exactly the filters of `GET /audit/events`; dates are whole UTC days with `to` sent as the next day's 00:00Z because `until` is exclusive; pages of 50 with the total; the CSV link carries the same query; "Write a retained export" posts since/until/action and shows location, retention date and SHA-256. *Consequences:* the screen, the CSV and the export can never disagree about the window.

### Integration (DEC-795 … DEC-798)

- **DEC-795 — Two parked settings ship; `monitoring.retraining` stays parked on the run form.** *Context:* DEC-074 parked `governance.retention_days`, `monitoring.performance_alert_drop_pct` and `monitoring.retraining` in `ADVISORY_PATHS`, so the Setup form showed them disabled as "Coming later — recorded with the run, not yet applied". Phase 4b makes all three live, but not all from the same place. *Decision:* remove the first two from `ADVISORY_PATHS`: the retention job (DEC-736) and outcome ingestion (DEC-772) both read the value in the run's own `run_config.json`, so the control on the form is exactly what applies. Keep `monitoring.retraining` parked on the form: it is read from the use case's configuration by the managed schedules (DEC-767), so a value moved on one run's form still changes nothing, and the note beside it stays true. The pinned test `test_exactly_the_parked_settings_are_marked_advisory` is updated to the new set, and a new test pins that the two shipped settings are live controls. *Consequences:* the recipe hash is unchanged for every recipe, because a `Recipe` never carried monitoring or governance; a one-line edit above a block in `engine/config.py` (trunk-owned), recorded in `docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md`.
- **DEC-796 — The consent seam reads the settings the API reads.** *Context:* the scoring seam (DEC-732) resolved its settings with the environment-only `settings()`, while the API uses `load_settings()`. A deployment's task sets only `MARKETING_AI_SETTINGS_SOURCE=aws` and keeps `metadata_backend`, the DSN and `client_id` in Parameter Store, so the seam would have found no ledger — and gated nothing — on exactly the deployments that need it, or hashed with a different salt from the API. *Decision:* `consent_gate_for_run` and `ledger_engine_for` fall back to `load_settings()`. *Consequences:* one Parameter Store read per gated scoring run; on a laptop `load_settings()` is `settings()`, so nothing changes there; a test pins the loader.
- **DEC-797 — Autogenerate stays scoped to `METADATA_TABLES`.** *Context:* `alembic/env.py` (Phase 4a) limits `make revision` to `METADATA_TABLES`, so Phase 4b tables are invisible to autogenerate. Widening `include_name` to `PLATFORM_TABLES` would be wrong on its own: `env.py` does not import the Phase 4b row classes, and a table in the filter but not in the metadata is one autogenerate proposes to drop. *Decision:* leave `env.py` as it is; Phase 4b's drift is checked by its own migration tests (`test_platform_migration.py`, `test_scheduling_migration.py`), and the change is requested from Phase 4a with both halves (import the row modules, then widen the filter). *Consequences:* until then, a Phase 4b schema change is written as a migration by hand, which is what `0002`–`0004` already are.
- **DEC-798 — `make production-test` runs every Phase 4b suite, slow ones included.** *Context:* the fast gate (`make test`) excludes the two tests that train a real model through a schedule, and the jsdom screen tests need their fixtures written by pytest. *Decision:* `production-test` runs `tests/unit/production`, `tests/integration/production` and the two migration-chain test files with only the paid markers excluded; `production-test-fast` adds `not slow`. The jsdom suites run inside pytest and skip, saying so, without node and jsdom. *Consequences:* one command answers "is Phase 4b green", and it needs no AWS account.

### Part 1 review fixes (DEC-707 … DEC-709, DEC-723 … DEC-728, DEC-737 … DEC-739, DEC-744, DEC-775 … DEC-779)

Three adversarial reviews (security, privacy, correctness) of Part 1 found the defects these entries
answer. Each has a regression test written first as the failing reproduction.

- **DEC-707 — A write or delete through `S3Storage` reaches its local mirror.** *Context:* `local_path` hydrates a key into a per-process mirror once; an erasure rewrote the object in the bucket while the same process kept serving the old bytes from the mirror to every later reader (a dataset, an index). *Decision:* a cross-branch, backward-compatible change to `engine/aws/s3_storage.py`: after `open_write` (and so `write_bytes`) commits, a mirror copy of that key, if one exists, is re-downloaded; `delete` removes the mirror copy and its hydration mark. The mirror's publish path (`_open_write`) is unchanged. *Consequences:* a moto test erases a hydrated dataset and reads `local_path` again.
- **DEC-708 — Lifecycle rules are owned per client prefix; old object versions are deleted by the job that rewrote them.** *Context:* `apply_lifecycle` treated every rule under `rule_id_prefix` as its own, so applying client B's rules dropped client A's; and the versioned artefact bucket kept erased and expired bytes as noncurrent versions under `runs/`, which no rule covered. *Decision:* an existing rule whose ID has the shape `<prefix><label>-(uploads|datasets|sources…)` with a *different* label is preserved; any other rule under the prefix is still replaced. Erasure and retention call `layout.purge_old_versions` after each rewrite or delete: on a store with versions (`S3Storage.purge_noncurrent_versions`, a cross-branch addition) the noncurrent versions of that key go, under `uploads/` and `runs/` only - the prefixes the deployment already grants `s3:DeleteObjectVersion` on (`infra.naming.ROW_LEVEL_PREFIXES`); elsewhere the lifecycle backstop's `NoncurrentVersionExpiration` applies. A failure to purge is logged, not raised. *Consequences:* moto tests for both.
- **DEC-709 — A model whose training data retention has deleted is flagged as "exposure unknown".** *Context:* exposure was inferred only from files still present; after `retention_days` a champion that learned from the person was never flagged. *Decision:* a training run with a model whose upload or dataset no longer exists is listed in `PrincipalFindings.models_exposure_unknown`; erasure flags those versions with reason `training_input_unavailable`, reports them in `ErasureOutcome.models_exposure_unknown`, and the request is `completed_with_exceptions`. *Consequences:* conservative - on an old deployment every erasure makes the current champion due at the next cycle, which retrains it on data that can be checked; the alternative (a durable hashed-key manifest per training run) is a later improvement, not a prerequisite.
- **DEC-723 — Approval and roles cannot be granted by the person they constrain.** *Context:* `governance.approval_required` is a per-run override, so an Analyst could send `false` on `POST /runs` and have the register stage crown their own model; and an Admin could `PATCH` their own account to add Approver. *Decision:* `api.access.require_roles_for_run_overrides` refuses a run whose merged configuration turns approval off where the use case requires it unless the caller holds Approver (403 `ROLE_REQUIRED`, "Only an Approver can turn off approval for a run.", audit `reason_code` `APPROVAL_OVERRIDE_REFUSED`); a run may still make approval stricter. `PATCH /users/{own id}` that *adds* a role is 409 `SELF_ROLE_GRANT` (dropping one's own role is allowed, subject to `LAST_ADMIN`). An Admin's reset of someone else's password is audited as its own action, `users.password_reset`, with the target's roles, and signs the target out everywhere. *Consequences:* the one-line check is a cross-branch call in `api/routes/runs.py` (imported lazily - `api.access` imports `api.routes.uploads`). Residual, stated: an Admin can still create a new account they control with Approver; the trail records who created it and with which roles, and a two-person rule belongs with the identity provider (P5, DEC-713).
- **DEC-724 — Anonymous audit events are rate-limited and cannot name their request id.** *Context:* every mutating request is audited, including unauthenticated ones to unmatched paths, into a table nothing may delete; and an anonymous caller could reuse one `X-Request-ID` for many events. *Decision:* at most `ANONYMOUS_EVENTS_PER_MINUTE` (120) events per client address per minute have no principal; the rest are dropped, counted on `app.state.audit_events_dropped`, with one WARNING per address and minute. An anonymous event whose request id the caller supplied is stored under a minted id with the caller's in `details.client_request_id` (a new `DETAIL_KEYS` entry); the response still echoes the caller's. A HEAD answered 405 read nothing and is not audited. *Consequences:* signed-in callers are never limited, so the trail of what principals did is complete; the limiter is per process.
- **DEC-725 — An open prod deployment says so at startup.** *Context:* the deployed `api.main:app` is built without settings, so the DEC-702 ERROR was written only at the first non-public request - never, on a deployment only the health probe reaches. *Decision:* `install_access` adds a startup handler that resolves the settings the way `api.deps.get_settings` does and calls the same warning. *Consequences:* tested against `create_app()` with `MARKETING_AI_ENV=prod` and `MARKETING_AI_AUTH_MODE=off`.
- **DEC-726 — The Object Lock audit copy is made on a schedule.** *Context:* exports happened only when an Admin asked, so the rows most in need of protection could be rewritten by the database owner before any immutable copy existed. *Decision:* `engine.audit.export.export_due` writes `[end of the last scheduled export, now)` once a day and appends one `audit.export.scheduled` event as `system:audit-export` with `window_start`, `window_end`, the key and the file's SHA-256 (new `DETAIL_KEYS`); consecutive files tile the log. It runs from the hourly **housekeeping** EventBridge schedule `marketing-ai-housekeeping` (`rate(1 hour)`, `scripts/fire_schedule.py --sweep --export-audit`), which the API creates or updates at every startup in its own group - no new infrastructure or grant - and from the local scheduler's tick. An export more than two days late is logged at ERROR, which the deployment's error alarm counts. *Consequences:* the unexported window is at most a day. A hash chain across rows (`prev_hash`) was considered and not built: appends come from several processes, and a chain needs a serialised writer the Postgres schema does not have; the daily locked copy is the control.
- **DEC-727 — The audit bucket has its own retained key.** *Context:* it was encrypted with the product key, which a non-prod `cdk destroy` schedules for deletion, leaving every COMPLIANCE-locked export unreadable 30 days later. *Decision:* `OperationsStack` creates `AuditExportKey` (rotation on, `RemovalPolicy.RETAIN` at every `env_name`); its key policy lets the API task role `kms:GenerateDataKey` only, so no other stack's policy changes. *Consequences:* one new `AWS::KMS::Key` in the operations snapshot; `tests/infra/test_phase4b_audit.py` now asserts the dedicated key instead of the product key.
- **DEC-728 — An erasure waits for runs still reading the person's data.** *Context:* a scoring or training job that had already read an upload or dataset would write scores and explanations containing the id after the erasure reported `completed`. *Decision:* before rewriting anything, `erase` finds non-terminal runs that read an affected upload, dataset (or its sources) or write into an affected run directory, and fails with `ERASURE_RUNS_IN_PROGRESS`; the request is recorded as failed and the API answers 409 ("ask again once it has finished"). *Consequences:* a run that starts during the erasure is still not coordinated with (DEC-749's limit); the Admin repeats the request after long-running jobs finish.
- **DEC-737 — A numeric id is matched only in key columns, and ids are compared in one canonical form.** *Context:* erasing `5` masked every non-key cell equal to 5, turned `2.5` into `2.[erased]`, deleted every row of a keyless file holding a 5, and exported other customers' whole rows; erasing `00104` missed the scores, where ingest had read the key as `104`. *Decision:* `principal_key` reads a digit-only key as its number (`00104` → `104`) everywhere - ledger hashes, the erasure register, matching. For an id that is a number, token matching is off, non-key cells are never masked, and a file with no recorded key is reported as `needs_review` (listed in `unrewritable_keys`, `completed_with_exceptions`, never rewritten, not evidence of training exposure, not extracted). JSON rows are decided by the file's key columns or a record's own `primary_key`/`entity_key`, and a profile's `preview_rows` by the key column's position. The access export returns only rows the same rule says are the person's. Parquet dictionary columns are rebuilt after a rewrite, and a dictionary entry nobody uses counts as an occurrence, so the rescan cannot pass while the id sits in the dictionary page. A tombstoned JSON row follows the table rule (text emptied, numbers kept). *Consequences:* `0104` and `104` are one principal wherever the column is digit-only, which is what ingest already made them.
- **DEC-738 — The consent gate fails closed and is the stricter of two records.** *Context:* the gate switched off whenever the run's client had no ledger - including a new client an Analyst onboarded, and every dataset run when consent was recorded under the deployment's own `client_id`; and a ledger grant overrode a fresher opt-out in the file. *Decision:* the gate uses the run's client's ledger, else the deployment client's; if neither exists but the deployment keeps a ledger for the purpose, the gate still applies to the run's client and nobody is contactable (the report says so). With a consent column, the verdict is ledger AND the file's value; `ConsentReport.excluded_file_opt_out` counts the difference. An access request or erasure that names no client exports or deletes consent history under every client. Scores and explanations of non-consented rows are still written, as Phase 1 does for `consent_false`: the gate governs contact, not measurement. *Consequences:* `test_consent_scoring.py`'s "another client changes nothing" and "the ledger replaces the column (30)" expectations were changed to these rules (32, and a fail-closed test).
- **DEC-739 — The job finds the ledger where the API wrote it.** *Context:* with a local artefact volume and Postgres metadata (docker-compose) the API wrote the ledger to Postgres and the scoring job looked for `platform.db` in the volume, gating nothing. *Decision:* `ledger_engine_for` uses SQLite under the store's root only when `metadata_backend == "sqlite"`, else `platform_engine(settings)`, the API's own choice.
- **DEC-744 — A run cannot extend retention, and 0 never means forever.** *Context:* a per-run `governance.retention_days` of 730 (an Analyst's override) kept an upload, its dataset and scores past the Admin's use-case setting; and with 0 an input no run ever read was kept forever. *Decision:* a run's value is `min(run, use case)`; inputs still take the largest of those. With 0, an unconsumed input is due after `ZERO_DAYS_GRACE` (1 day).
- **DEC-775 — A slot EventBridge never delivered is recorded when a later one arrives.** *Context:* `fire_scheduled` moved `next_due_at` past the slot it fired, so a dropped earlier slot became invisible to every sweep. *Decision:* before moving it, `fire_scheduled` records as missed every slot in `[next_due_at, slot)` older than the grace (30 minutes, `EVENTBRIDGE_GRACE`); a younger one may still be delivered, so `next_due_at` stops at it for the sweep. The hourly housekeeping schedule (DEC-726) sweeps even when no target runs.
- **DEC-776 — Every managed schedule is pushed on every sync.** *Context:* a failed push left a row EventBridge never heard of, and later syncs skipped it as unchanged. *Decision:* unchanged managed schedules are pushed again (`Scheduler.sync` is create-or-update).
- **DEC-777 — An erasure flag is answered even when drift cannot be measured.** *Context:* the drift check returned before reading the flags when there was no champion, no scored run or no measurable drift. *Decision:* flags are read first; under `on_drift`, any open flag of the use case starts a `RETRAIN_FOR_ERASURE` run in those cases too.
- **DEC-778 — A scheduler-started training run always waits for an Approver.** *Context:* a use case with `approval_required: false` let a scheduled retrain crown itself. *Decision:* the firer resolves training runs with the override `governance.approval_required: true` (recorded as an override in `run_config.json`).
- **DEC-779 — One catch-up, one missed alert, one failed alert.** *Context:* two processes sweeping one backlog each claimed some slots and each caught up and alerted; two settlers of one failed firing each alerted. *Decision:* only the process that claimed the newest missed slot runs the catch-up and raises the alert, which counts every missed slot; settling is a conditional update (`WHERE status = 'running'`, `ScheduleStore.settle_firing`) and only the winner alerts.

## Phase 3b — Uplift modelling (DEC-600 … DEC-699)

Plan B assigns Phase 3b the range DEC-600 … DEC-699 because it reserves the DEC-500s for Phase 5;
the claim row is in `PARALLEL_WORK_PROTOCOL.md` §4. DEC-600 … DEC-603 and DEC-605 were cited in code
before they were written down here; they are recorded as the code already describes them. DEC-610
onwards record the builders' decisions from milestones M40 to M45; where a later fix changed one, the
entry that changed it is named. The plain-language guide is [`UPLIFT.md`](UPLIFT.md).

- **DEC-600 — Phase 3b's shared-file blocks come last, after Phase 4a's, and its numbers start at 600.** *Context:* `PARALLEL_WORK_PROTOCOL.md` §4 gives each branch a marked block in every shared file, and `tests/unit/test_shared_file_markers.py` pins their order. Phase 3b started after Phases 2, 3a and 4a had merged, so there is no gap for its block in phase-number order, and plan B reserves the DEC-500s for Phase 5. *Decision:* every shared file gets a `PHASE-3B (uplift)` block appended after the `PHASE-4A` one, and the marker test checks the order Phase 2, 3a, 4a, 3b. Decisions take DEC-600 … DEC-699, claimed by a row in the §4 table. *Consequences:* "append at the end of your block" still means one place to every reader; the DEC-500s stay free for the phase the plan names.
- **DEC-601 — Uplift is a problem type of its own, configured by one `uplift:` block that lives outside `engine/config.py`.** *Context:* plan B §6 adds a problem type, a metric and a config block. `_Base` forbids unknown keys, so a use case cannot carry `uplift:` until `UseCaseConfig` has the field; and Phase 2's `UseCaseConfig.model_rebuild()` runs above the Phase 3b block, so a type defined in the block could not be referenced. *Decision:* the types live in `engine/uplift/config.py`, which imports nothing from `engine`; `engine/config.py` gains exactly four declarations above the block - `ProblemType.UPLIFT`, `Metric.AUUC`, `UseCaseConfig.uplift` (defaulted) and the uplift paths in `EXTRA_OVERRIDABLE_PATHS` - and `configs/engine.yaml` gains the `auuc` metric, the `uplift` problem type and the defaulted `defaults.uplift` block. Every uplift field is read only when `problem_type` is `uplift`, so no other run moves. `Pipeline.run_train` and `run_score` each gain one line asking `uplift_flow_for` (in the pipeline's Phase 3b block) first. All of it is announced in `docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md`. *Consequences:* a classification or regression run executes exactly the code it did before; the uplift block is overridable per run like `target.column`, except where DEC-607 says otherwise.
- **DEC-602 — Uplift artefacts have their own registry and their own route.** *Context:* `engine.contracts.ARTEFACT_REGISTRY` is an immutable mapping built above the shared-file blocks and pinned name for name by a test. *Decision:* `engine.uplift.contracts.UPLIFT_ARTEFACTS` maps each uplift filename to its model, and `GET /runs/{run_id}/uplift/{name}` serves only those names - a whitelist, not a path join. *Consequences:* the Phase 1 registry and its test are untouched; the UI reads uplift artefacts from one route.
- **DEC-603 — Uplift checks are `UpliftCheck`, a twin of `ValidationCheck` with their own code table.** *Context:* `ValidationCheck` accepts only the Phase 1 code table, which tests pin at nineteen codes; Phase 2 met the same wall (DEC-101). *Decision:* `UpliftCheck` carries `ValidationCheck`'s fields plus `acknowledgeable`, and `UPLIFT_VALIDATION_CODES` lists the six plan B §4 codes. `docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md` asks for one code registry behind all three check models. *Consequences:* one more duplicated field list, recorded so it is closed deliberately.
- **DEC-604 — Expected incremental conversions ask the hold-out about the ranking depth the selection reaches, not about `N / rows`.** *Context:* the recommendation reports `N × observed uplift in the top share of the hold-out`. On a scoring run, control and suppressed rows are ranked but never chosen, so the `N` chosen rows reach further down the ranking than the top `N`: with a 10% control group and 40% suppressed, about twice as far. Asking the hold-out about its top `N / rows` asked about a smaller, better-ranked share; a review simulation with a steep uplift curve reported 346 expected conversions (95% CI 313 to 391) against a true 272. *Decision:* `recommend_policy` asks about `d / rows`, where `d` is the position of the last chosen row in the ranking of every scored row (`engine.uplift.policy.ranking`). On a training hold-out every row is eligible and `d = N`, so nothing changes there. *Consequences:* the same simulation now reports 260 (235 to 281) against 272; eligibility that does not depend on uplift - the random control group - is handled exactly, and suppression correlated with uplift is handled as well as a rank share can.
- **DEC-605 — Uplift intervals are percentile bootstraps drawn within each arm, with the model held fixed.** *Context:* plan B asks for confidence intervals on AUUC, the Qini coefficient, the ATE and uplift@k, and the champion rule needs an AUUC lower bound. *Decision:* each resample draws with replacement within the treated arm and within the control arm (arm sizes fixed), predictions are not refitted, the draws come from `np.random.default_rng(seed)` in a documented order, and the 95% interval is the 2.5th to 97.5th percentile. The bootstrap is vectorised in chunks. *Consequences:* the interval measures the noise of the evaluation, which is what "lower bound above zero" needs; 200 resamples of 30,000 rows take seconds.
- **DEC-606 — Rows of equal predicted uplift are ordered by a run-seeded per-customer hash, and `intended_treatment` is a ranking position.** *Context:* `intended_treatment` marked the selected rows plus the control rows with uplift at or above the lowest selected uplift, and ties kept input order. When a coarse model gives a block of customers the same uplift and the budget cuts through it, only the first part of the block in file order was treated, but every tied control row was "intended". With a file sorted by how likely each customer converts anyway, a campaign with no effect measured a lift of 28 points (p = 1e-36). *Decision:* a scoring run breaks ties with `sha256("uplift-tie:{seed_from(run_id)}:{primary key}")`, salted differently from the control-group draw, in both the selection and the intended mask. A held-out row is intended when it is a persuadable ranked at or above the last selected row in that one ranking. Without a tie-break (a training hold-out, the pure functions) ties keep input order, as before. *Consequences:* the treated and held-out parts of a tie block are both random draws from it, so the comparison is like with like again (the same case now measures no effect), and the choice does not depend on the file's row order.
- **DEC-607 — `uplift.randomness_auc_max` cannot be overridden per run.** *Context:* DEC-601 made every uplift leaf overridable per run. A request carrying `uplift.randomness_auc_max: 1.0` then passed a targeted campaign through TREATMENT_NOT_RANDOM, and every artefact said `causal: true`. Plan B §4 says non-random assignment must never be called causal, and acknowledging the finding (which runs the model and marks it not causal) is the honest way past it. *Decision:* `UPLIFT_RUN_LOCKED_PATHS = {"uplift.randomness_auc_max"}` is removed from `UPLIFT_OVERRIDABLE_PATHS`, so the override is refused with `OVERRIDE_UNKNOWN_PATH` (422). The threshold can still be changed in the use-case file, where it is a reviewed change. The arm-size floors stay overridable: a small arm only widens the intervals, and the champion rule already refuses what they cannot show. *Consequences:* supersedes the "every leaf" part of DEC-601; the per-run acknowledgement is the only per-run way past the check.
- **DEC-608 — Uplift is not a Phase 1 Setup choice.** *Context:* the catalog marked `uplift` `enabled: true`, so Phase 1's Setup offered it in every use case. Choosing it started an uplift run through Phase 1's `POST /runs`, with no treatment picker and none of the synchronous uplift checks behind `POST /uplift/runs`. Non-random data then failed later with a generic `RUN_BLOCKED_BY_VALIDATION`, and a finished run left Phase 1's Model page looking for an `evaluation.json` that uplift does not write. *Decision:* the catalog marks `uplift` `enabled: false`, so the Phase 1 Setup lists it disabled, like forecasting and clustering. Uplift runs start from `#/uplift`, which the uplift module links from the Overview. `trainable_in_phase_1` only affects display, so no uplift path depends on the flag. *Consequences:* Phase 1's Setup behaves as it did before Phase 3b. An API caller can still send `problem_type: uplift` to `POST /runs`; the run's own validate stage runs the uplift checks and refuses non-random data, and a request to refuse it synchronously is in `docs/CROSS_BRANCH_REQUESTS.md`.
- **DEC-609 — An uplift run fills an empty champion slot only on a use case configured as uplift.** *Context:* the registry keeps one champion per use case. A Phase 1 scoring run with no model named scores whatever holds the slot (`uplift_flow_for`), and the frozen Phase 1 rule never promotes a ROC AUC challenger over an AUUC champion (`METRIC_MISMATCH`). An uplift run started from `#/uplift` on a use case configured for classification crowned itself when the slot was empty. That use case's Phase 1 scoring then switched to uplift, and none of its classification models could be promoted again. *Decision:* `UpliftTrainFlow` auto-promotes into an empty slot only when `uplift_owns_champion_slot(resolved)` holds: the configured problem type is uplift and it did not come from a run override. Otherwise the model stays a candidate, and the reason says to promote it on the Models page to hand the slot over deliberately. `POST /uplift/runs` does not override `problem_type` on a use case already configured as uplift. Once a person has given the slot to an uplift model, later uplift runs are compared with it under the uplift champion rule. *Consequences:* Phase 1 behaviour of a classification use case cannot change without a person's decision. The uplift Setup screen offers every uplift version, champion or not, and a scoring run can name any of them, so a candidate is fully usable.
- **DEC-610 — The bootstrap is vectorised over ranked positions, and its draws do not depend on how it is chunked.** *Context:* DEC-605 needs 200 resamples of a 30,000-row hold-out, each re-ranked and walked to the end, and a Python loop over resamples takes minutes. *Decision:* each resample is a row of ranked positions of the hold-out. Treated and control rows are drawn separately, so arm sizes stay fixed. One `np.sort(axis=1)` per chunk re-ranks the resamples, `t` and `y` are gathered from the ranked arrays, and cumulative sums along the rows give `N_t`, `N_c`, `Y_t`, `Y_c` and every curve for all resamples at once. Chunks are capped at 2,000,000 cells, about 16 MB per working array. The draws are made one resample at a time from `np.random.default_rng(seed)`, treated arm first. *Consequences:* 200 resamples of 30,000 rows take about 1.4 s; the result for a given seed is the same whatever the chunk size.
- **DEC-611 — A bootstrap interval is null when any resample leaves the statistic undefined.** *Context:* a resample can leave a statistic undefined, for example a top 10% with no control row. Dropping those resamples would bias the interval towards the resamples that happened to be defined and claim a precision nobody measured. *Decision:* `ConfidenceValue.ci_low`/`ci_high` are `None` in that case, as the contract says an interval is when it cannot be computed. The UI shows "—", and the champion rule treats a missing lower bound as not measurable. *Consequences:* a thin hold-out shows gaps rather than a narrow, invented band.
- **DEC-612 — `bootstrap_uplift_at` shares the resamples of `evaluate_uplift`.** *Context:* the policy recommendation's "expected incremental conversions" interval and `uplift_evaluation.json`'s uplift@k interval describe the same quantity when they ask about the same share. *Decision:* both functions use the same seed and the same draw order, so for the same share they return the same interval (tested). *Consequences:* the Model and Output pages cannot show two different intervals for one number.
- **DEC-613 — The metrics break ties in predicted uplift by input order.** *Context:* the interface fixes a stable mergesort on `-pred`. A tie rule is needed for every ranking, and a random shuffle would make metrics non-deterministic. *Decision:* equal predictions keep their input order in `engine/uplift/metrics.py`, and each resample inherits the rule through the rows' input positions. A model that predicts one value for everyone is therefore scored as the input order, deterministically. The scoring-time tie-break of DEC-606 is a different rule for a different problem (who gets contacted) and does not change the metrics. *Consequences:* every metric is a pure function of its three arrays.
- **DEC-614 — The uplift champion rule checks causality and a positive AUUC lower bound before the same-hold-out check, and computes no percentage over a non-positive champion.** *Context:* plan B §5 adds two gates to Phase 1's rule. A relative improvement over an AUUC of zero or less is a division by nothing or a sign flip. *Decision:* `decide_uplift_champion` refuses a not-causal challenger, then one whose AUUC `ci_low` is missing or ≤ 0, each for its own stated reason, before it compares hold-out counts. When the champion's AUUC is ≤ 0, any strictly greater AUUC wins and `improvement_pct` is `None`. Different row, treated or control counts raise `ValueError`. This mirrors DEC-035 in the Phase 1 rule, which is untouched. *Consequences:* the reason a model was refused is always the most fundamental one.
- **DEC-615 — The X-learner on LightGBM is explained by exact SHAP; every other learner by a surrogate.** *Context:* plan B §5 asks for SHAP on the uplift estimate. LightGBM TreeSHAP of a classifier is additive in log-odds, not probability, so the S- and T-learners' uplift (a difference of probabilities) has no exact TreeSHAP, and AutoGluon ensembles have none at all. *Decision:* the X-learner's uplift `e·τ0(x) + (1−e)·τ1(x)` is linear in two LightGBM regressors, so the same blend of their `pred_contrib` outputs is the exact SHAP of the uplift. The other learners are explained through a LightGBM regressor surrogate fitted to the predicted uplift. *Consequences:* the explanation is never implied to be exact when it is not (DEC-616).
- **DEC-616 — `explanation_fidelity` is the R² of the SHAP row sums against the predicted uplift, for every learner.** *Context:* the exact and surrogate paths need one comparable number. *Decision:* it is computed, never asserted: about 1.0 for the exact X-learner on LightGBM, the surrogate's in-sample R² otherwise, `None` when the predicted uplift is constant. The feature-importance caption says when a surrogate explains the model and gives its R². *Consequences:* a reader can see how faithful the "why" is.
- **DEC-617 — AutoGluon sub-predictors are saved as directories beside the pickle, not inside it.** *Context:* AutoGluon reloads its models by path, so pickling a `TabularPredictor` is not reliable. *Decision:* the pickle stores only each sub-model's directory name (`mu0`, `mu1`, `tau0`, `tau1`, `s_model`). `save_model` copies the directories beside `uplift_learner.pkl` and `load_model` re-attaches them. *Consequences:* a model directory is self-contained and can be moved; a slow test deletes the work directory and moves the saved one to check this.
- **DEC-618 — A learner refuses to fit an empty arm or an arm whose outcome never varies.** *Context:* without both outcome classes in an arm there is no outcome model for that arm, and any uplift it produced would be invented. *Decision:* `fit` raises `ValueError`. `TREATMENT_ARM_TOO_SMALL` should stop such data earlier; this is the last line of defence. *Consequences:* no model is built on an arm that cannot support one.
- **DEC-619 — A scored row whose contributions are all zero gets no reasons.** *Context:* Phase 1 reason columns expect up to `top_n` entries per row. *Decision:* such a row is indistinguishable from the average as far as the model can tell, so `reasons=()` and the export tolerates empty reason slots. There is no "general" fallback reason. *Consequences:* a blank reason cell means "nothing to say", never a made-up sentence.
- **DEC-620 — LightGBM sub-models run with `deterministic=True`, `force_row_wise=True` and `n_jobs=2`.** *Context:* the champion rule compares models on one hold-out and a test compares save/load predictions; both need reproducible trees. *Decision:* these settings make the same data and seed give identical trees whatever the thread count, and a test asserts that two fits with the same seed predict identically. *Consequences:* a little speed is traded for reproducibility.
